Chow-Witt Rings of Classifying Spaces of Products of Multiplicative and Cyclic groups
Andrea Lachmann

Abstract




Abstract.

We compute the total Chow-Witt rings of the classifying space
B\mu_{n}
of the roots of unity, as well as the products
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
and
B\mu_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
for all
m,n\geq 1
based on the strategy by di Lorenzo and Mantovani (2023) for
B\mu_{n}
with
n
even.
Moreover we compute the total
I^{j}
-cohomology and Chow-Witt rings of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
for all
q,r\geq 1
and of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Chapter 1
Introduction

Chow-Witt groups have gained increasing attention in intersection theory and enumerative geometry over the past 20 years.
Classically, a central concept of intersection theory are Chow groups of a scheme containing information about the subschemes in a given dimension.
Another invariant of schemes is the Witt ring of quadratic forms over a scheme introduced by
[
Kne77
, Chapter I §5]
.
Chow-Witt groups were then conceived by
[
BM00
]
via a fiber product of Chow and Witt theory, and implemented in technical detail by
[
Fas05
]
.

One object of interest in this area are characteristic classes of vector bundles like Chern classes, Euler classes and Stiefel-Whitney classes living in the different theories mentioned above.
In topology the Euler class assigns to a real vector bundle an element in the singular cohomology of the base space with a local coefficient system (integral coefficients if the vector bundle is orientable).
One of its properties is being an obstruction for the vector bundle to split off a trivial summand, meaning if such a splitting exists then the Euler class vanishes.
Now for vector bundles over a scheme, one can of course consider characteristic classes with values in Chow groups, but it turns out that these satisfy the classical obstruction property only for
\mathbb{C}
-schemes.
For schemes over general fields and in particular over
\mathbb{R}
a characteristic class with this obstruction property lives in the Chow-Witt groups (compare
Theorem

2.1
).

Motivic classifying spaces of algebraic groups were introduced by
[
MV99
]
, imitating the long-familiar concept of classifying spaces in topology.
The étale and geometric classifying space classify
G
-bundles in the sense that according to
[
MV99
, §4 Proposition 1.15]
in the Nisnevich local motivic homotopy category of schemes, the set of maps from some
X
into the classifying space of
G
is in bijection to the set of isomorphism classes of
G
-torsors over
X
.
Around the same time Totaro independently defined the Chow ring of a classifying space by means of scheme approximations
[
Tot99
]
, without defining the classifying space itself which usually does not exist in the category of schemes.
This turned out to agree with the definition of Morel-Voevodsky and provides an important tool for computing Chow rings which was later extended to Chow-Witt groups by
[
AF16
, Theorem 3.3]
.

Some examples for computations of Chow-Witt rings include projective space
\mathbb{P}^{n}
by
[
Fas13
]
, which also provides a powerful tool for
I^{j}
-cohomology of projective bundles.
The ring structure for
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
was computed by
[
Wen24
]
as a special case of a Grassmannian.
Later
[
LM23
]
computed the Chow-Witt ring of
B\mu_{n}
for even
n
.
We will heavily build on these two articles in this work.
Further examples can be found in
[
HW19
]
and
[
HXZ20
]
.

In this work we aim to compute several new examples for Chow-Witt rings of classifying spaces of groups and products thereof.
We will extend the argument of
[
LM23
]
, considering
B\mu_{n}
as a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-bundle over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
and then applying a localization sequence to deduce the Chow-Witt groups of the former from those of the latter, to
n
odd (
Theorem

3.8
):

Satz A
.

Let
n
be an odd natural number and
k
a perfect field of characteristic coprime to
2
and
n
.
Denote by
\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(X)
the total Chow-Witt ring of a smooth scheme
X
.
Then there is an isomorphism of graded
\GW(k)
-algebras

\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})\cong\GW(k)[e]/(I(k)\cdot e,n\cdot e)

where
e\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(1))
denotes the Euler class of
\mathcal{O}_{B\mu_{n}}(-1)
.

Then we compute the
I^{j}
-cohomology of
\mathbb{P}^{m}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}
(
Theorem

4.5
) using the projective bundle formula of
[
Fas13
]
,
then combine this with the Chow groups of the same space (
[
Tot14
, Theorem 2.10, 2.12]
) via a fiber product statement of
[
HW19
, Lemma 2.11]
, see
Corollary

5.2
and
Corollary

5.3
.

Satz B
.

For
k
a perfect field of characteristic coprime to
2
, there is an isomorphism of graded
\GW(k)
-algebras

\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\GW(k)[e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}]/(I(k)\cdot(H_{1},H_{2},H_{3},e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}),\\
e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}+H_{3}e_{1}e_{2}-e_{3}^{2},\mathcal{J})

where
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
and
e_{3}
correspond to the Euler classes of
\mathcal{O}(1,0)
,
\mathcal{O}(0,1)
,
\mathcal{O}(1,1)
, respectively, the
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
,
H_{3}
are a kind of analogue of the hyperbolic form
h
living in the twisted degree
0
groups, and
\mathcal{J}
is a relation ideal. These are described in more detail in
Corollary

5.2
.

Later we will use a very similar strategy like for a single copy of
B\mu_{n}
to deduce the Chow-Witt rings of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
(
Theorem

6.2
) and
B\mu_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
(
Theorem

7.3
) from that of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
For some values of
m
and
n
this argument is inconclusive for degree
0
which is why in these cases we employ a comparison with Witt cohomology, which in turn can be computed via a Künneth formula developed by
[
HMW24
, Theorem 4.7]
.
Most of the ring structure can be derived from that of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
by precisely understanding the isomorphisms identifying those Chow-Witt groups whose twists become isomorphic over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
respectively
B\mu_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
.
These rings depend on the parity of
m
and
n
.
Their algebra presentations are so lengthy in total that we decided against including them in the introduction and the reader should refer to the respective theorems for details.

It will turn out that some but not all of the products considered - namely
\mathbb{P}^{m}\times\mathbb{P}^{n}
,
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
,
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
,
B\mu_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
- satisfy a Künneth isomorphism for Chow-Witt rings, see
Remark

7.5
.
This provides examples to the already known fact that such an isomorphism cannot be true for Chow-Witt rings of general products.
It remains an open question to establish a nice set of conditions under which this holds.
Such conditions are known for both Chow rings
[
Tot14
, Theorem 2.12]
and Witt cohomology
[
HMW24
, Proposition 4.7]
, but not for
I^{j}
-cohomology or Chow-Witt rings.

Notation and Conventions

Throughout all of this paper, let
k
be a
perfect field of characteristic not
2
.
Perfectness is relevant for some motivic homotopy arguments, for example avoiding smoothness issues since every regular finite type scheme over a perfect field is already smooth.
There are methods to circumvent this condition in many applications developed by
[
EK20
]
, and it might be possible that it can be entirely removed, see also
[
HMW24
, Section 2.3]
.
Characteristic different from
2
is a common assumption in the theory of quadratic forms, because quadratic forms agreeing with symmetric bilinear forms simplifies matters.
Recent works, e.g.
[
Fel20
]
, accomplish at least the construction of Chow-Witt groups as well as functoriality and long exact localization sequences in arbitrary characteristic.
However there remain arguments, such as the decomposition of Chow-Witt groups into Chow groups and
I^{j}
-cohomology (
Section

3
) and the projective bundle theorem for
I^{j}
-cohomology (
Proposition

4.2
), that are only known to be true in characteristic unequal
2
.

All
rings
are
associative and unital
.
All
schemes
are considered to be
separated of finite type over
k
.
This implies that the image of a closed subscheme under a proper morphism of schemes is again closed, which is necessary to construct a pushforward map on Chow-Witt groups. When working with cohomological Chow-Witt groups or any other kind of cohomology, that is, from
Section

5
onward, all schemes are required to be smooth.
For a scheme
X
and an integer
i
denote by
X_{(i)}
the set of points of dimension
i
in
X
, i.e. points whose closure has dimension
i
.
If
X
is smooth and thus has a fixed dimension, denote by
X^{(i)}
the set of points of codimension
i
.

For a commutative ring
A
and symbols
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
, we denote by
A\langle x_{1},x_{2},\ldots\rangle
the free
A
-module generated by
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
, not to be confused with the polynomial ring
A[x_{1},x_{2},\ldots]
.

We use the following standard notation.

\GW(k)

Grothendieck-Witt ring of quadratic forms over
k

\witt(k)

Witt ring of quadratic forms modulo hyperbolic spaces

I(k)

the fundamental ideal of
\GW(k)
or
\witt(k)

\chow_{i}(X)
,
\chow^{i}(X)

i
-th (co)homological Chow group of a scheme
X

\chowmodtwo_{i}(X)
,
\chowmodtwo^{i}(X)

i
-th (co)homological Chow group modulo
2

\chowwitt_{i}(X,\mathcal{L})
,
\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})

i
-th (co)homological Chow-Witt group twisted by a line

bundle
\mathcal{L}
over
X

K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(k)

the graded Milnor-Witt K-theory ring

K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(k)

the graded Milnor K-theory ring

I^{*}(k)=K^{\mathrm{W}}_{*}(k)

the graded ring consisting of powers of
I(k)
, where

I^{\leq 0}(k)\coloneqq\witt(k)

All of these will be introduced in detail in
Chapter

2
.

Chapter 2
Chow-Witt Rings

This chapter introduces Chow-Witt groups and rings and some of their fundamental properties.
In some places we will only sketch ideas but do not include proofs, and the reader should be aware that some results cited from the literature are highly non-trivial.

1
Motivation and Relation to Chow Groups

The Chow-Witt ring is a quadratic refinement of the Chow ring, so to provide some context we will start with a quick recollection on the latter.
A more detailed account can be found e.g. in
[
Ful98
, Chapter 1]
.

Let
X
be a scheme over
k
.
The group of algebraic
i
-cycles of
X
is the free abelian group on closed subschemes of
X
of dimension
i
, and the
i
-th Chow group
\chow_{i}(X)
is then obtained by dividing out rational equivalence.
This can be expressed by the exact sequence

\bigoplus_{W\in X_{(i+1)}}\kappa(W)^{\times}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{div}}\bigoplus_{V\in X_{(i)}}\mathbb{Z}\langle V\rangle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chow_{i}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0\,.

The idea of Chow-Witt groups
\chowwitt_{i}(X)
is to consider algebraic cycles
\sum n_{V}[V]
not with integral coefficients
n_{V}
, but instead coefficients in the Grothendieck-Witt ring
\GW(k)
, the group completion of quadratic forms over
k
up to isometry, equipped with direct sum and tensor product.
This is motivated by the fact that the Grothendieck-Witt ring captures the arithmetic of the base field in more detail.
Through this one hopes to generalize certain results that hold only over the base field
\mathbb{C}
in classical intersection theory (using Chow groups), considering that
\GW(\mathbb{C})\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
For example, the zeroth motivic stable stem is isomorphic to
\GW(k)
by
[
Mor03
, 6.4.1]
whereas its topological analogue is
\pi_{0}^{s}(\mathbb{S})\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
A further example is the following splitting theorem of Morel, matching the obstruction property of topological Euler classes.

Satz 2.1

(
[
Mor12
, Thm 8.14]
)
.

Assume
r\geq 4
.
Let
X
be a smooth affine k-scheme of dimension
\leq r
, and let
\xi
be an oriented algebraic vector bundle of rank
r
.
Then:

\text{$\xi$ splits off a trivial line bundle}\Leftrightarrow\eulercw(\xi)=0\in\chowwitt^{r}(X)

The analogous statement for Chern classes under the assumption that
k
is algebraically closed was proved by
[
Mur94
, Theorem 3.8]
.
This obstruction property for vector bundles to split off a trivial summand is also true for Euler classes of topological spaces which take values in singular cohomology with integral coefficients.
In fact, there is also a direct connection between
I^{j}
-cohomology, which is a quotient of Chow-Witt groups, and singular cohomology:
For a smooth scheme
X
over a field
k\supseteq\mathbb{R}
and a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
over
X
, Jacobson
[
Jac17
]
defines a real cycle class map

H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}_{\mathrm{sing}}(X(\mathbb{R});\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{L}))

where
X(\mathbb{R})
denotes the set of real points of
X
with the analytic topology.
This is an isomorphism if
j>\dim X

[
Jac17
, Corollary 8.3]
, or if
j\geq i
and
X
is cellular
[
HWXZ21
, Theorem 5.7]
.

In order to do construct such a quadratic refinement it will be helpful to rephrase the above definition of Chow groups in terms of Milnor K-theory.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated field extension over
k
.
Its Milnor K-theory
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)
is the graded-commutative ring generated by the symbols
[a]
for all
a\in F^{\times}
in degree
1
, modulo the relations
[a][1-a]
for all
a\neq 0,\,1
and
[a]+[b]=[ab]
for all
a,b\in F^{\times}
.
The
i
-th degree of this ring is the
i
-th Milnor K-theory group of
F
, denoted
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{i}(F)
.

Observing that we have
F^{\times}=K^{\mathrm{M}}_{1}(F)
,
\mathbb{Z}=K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(F)
and
0=K^{\mathrm{M}}_{-1}(F)
, the exact sequence defining the
i
-th Chow group above can be expressed using the chain complex

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(i+1)}}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{1}(\kappa(x))\xrightarrow{\operatorname{div}}\bigoplus_{y\in X_{(i)}}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(\kappa(y))\xrightarrow{div}\bigoplus_{z\in X_{(i-1)}}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{-1}(\kappa(z))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

known as the Gersten complex
C_{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*},i)
and the
i
-th Chow group is the homology at the middle term.
Naively we want to replace
K^{\mathrm{M}}
in the above chain complex by its
“quadratic refinement”
, Milnor-Witt K-theory
K^{\mathrm{MW}}
:

Definition 2.3
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated field extension over
k
.
Its Milnor-Witt K-theory
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)
is the graded (non-commutative) ring generated by the symbols

1.

[a]
for all
a\in F^{\times}
in degree
1
,

2.

\eta
in degree
-1

modulo the relations

1.

[a]\cdot[1-a]
for
a\neq 0,\,1
,

2.

[a]+[b]+\eta\cdot[a]\cdot[b]-[ab]
for
a,\,b\in F^{\times}
,

3.

\eta\cdot[a]-[a]\cdot\eta
for
a\in F^{\times}
,

4.

\eta^{2}\cdot[-1]+2\eta
.

The
i
-th degree of this ring is the
i
-th Milnor-Witt K-theory group of
F
, which is denoted by
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{i}(F)
.

In this setup, however, it is more difficult to define the map
\operatorname{div}
.
This will be the task of the next section.

This construction of Milnow-Witt K-theory as a quadratic refinement of Milnor K-theory is partly justified by the following result of Morel.

Proposition 2.4

(
[
Mor12
, 3.10]
)
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated field extension over
k
.
For
a\in F^{\times}
, denote by
\langle a\rangle
the
1
-dimensional quadratic form over
k
defined by
(x,y)\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}a\cdot x\cdot y
.
Then the assignment

\displaystyle\GW(k)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{0}(F)

\displaystyle\langle a\rangle

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}1+\eta\cdot[a]

extends to a well defined ring isomorphism.

Beispiel 2.5
.

1.

The Grothendieck-Witt ring of any quadratically closed field is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}

[
Lam05
, II.3.1]
.

2.

\GW(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]

[
Lam05
, II.3.2]
.

In view of this isomorphism we will denote the elements
1+\eta[a]
in
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{0}(F)
by
\langle a\rangle
.
In particular, the hyperbolic form
h=\langle 1\rangle+\langle-1\rangle=1+\langle-1\rangle\in\GW(F)
maps to
2+\eta[-1]
and we will denote this element by
h
as well.
Note that under this identification the last relation from
Definition

2.3
becomes
\eta h
, and the second relation guarantees
\langle ab\rangle=\langle a\rangle\langle b\rangle
.

There are two obvious maps comparing Milnor and Milnor-Witt K-theory:

Definition 2.6
.

Multiplication with the hyperbolic form
h
defines a map of graded
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)
-modules

h\colon K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)

called the hyperbolic map.
Dividing out the ideal generated by the element
\eta
defines a graded ring epimorphism

\rho\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)

which we will call the reduction map.

The composition
\rho\circ h
equals multiplication with
\rho(h)=\rho(2+\eta[-1])=2
.
Both
h
and
\rho
will extend to maps comparing Chow and Chow-Witt rings.

Beispiel 2.7
.

1.

For a quadratically closed field, both
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}
and
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{0}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
.
The hyperbolic map is given by multiplication with
2
, and the reduction map is the identity.

2.

The Grothendieck-Witt group of
\mathbb{R}
is isomorphic to the group ring
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
with the elements of
\mathbb{Z}/2
corresponding to the quadratic forms
\langle 1\rangle
and
\langle-1\rangle
.
The hyperbolic map is given by multiplication with the hyperbolic form
\langle 1\rangle+\langle-1\rangle
,
and the reduction map is the
\mathbb{Z}
-linear extension of the map sending both
\langle 1\rangle
and
\langle-1\rangle
to
1\in K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{Z}

2
The Gersten-Witt Complex

In this section we follow the exposition of
[
Fas20
]
(the original construction is due to
[
Fas08
]
) to define a family of chain complexes where the
i
-th degree of the
j
-th complex is

C_{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(i)}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j+i}(\kappa(x))

and the differentials mimic the divisor map
\operatorname{div}
.
Due to its technical complexity the explicit description of this chain complex is rarely used in computations, as opposed to the tools introduced in
Sections

3
and
5
.

First let
F
be a finitely generated field extension over the base field
k
and
\nu\colon F\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
a discrete valuation.
Denote by
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}
the valuation ring,
\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}
its maximal ideal and
\kappa(\nu)=\mathcal{O}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}
its residue field, and choose a uniformizing parameter, i.e. a generator
\pi
of
\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}
.
For
u\in\mathcal{O}_{\nu}
, denote the image of
u
in
\kappa(\nu)
by
\overline{u}
.

Satz 2.8

(Morel)
.

Under the above conditions, there exists a unique morphism of graded abelian groups

\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(\nu))

satisfying

1.

\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}([\pi]\cdot[u_{2}]\cdot\ldots\cdot[u_{n}])=[\overline{u_{2}}]\cdot\ldots\cdot[\overline{u_{n}}]
,

2.

\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}([u_{1}]\cdot\ldots\cdot[u_{n}])=0
,

3.

\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}(\eta\cdot\alpha)=\eta\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}(\alpha)

for any
u_{1},\ldots u_{n}\in\mathcal{O}_{\nu}^{\times}
and
\alpha\in K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)
.

Proof.

See
[
Mor12
, 3.15]
.
The idea is to define an auxilary map into the polynomial ring

\displaystyle\Theta^{\pi}_{\nu}\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathcal{O}_{\nu}^{\times}=F^{\times}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(\kappa(\nu))[x]/(x^{2})/(x^{2}-x\cdot[-1])

\displaystyle(\pi^{n}\cdot u)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}[\overline{u}]+\left(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\langle(-1)^{i}\rangle\langle\overline{u}\rangle\right)x

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\eta

where the symbol
x
lives in degree
1
.
Then check that this is compatible with the relations of Milnor-Witt K-theory and thus can be extended to a graded ring homomorphism on
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)
.
Now set

s^{\pi}_{\nu}(\alpha)+\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}(\alpha)x\coloneqq\Theta^{\pi}_{\nu}(\alpha)

and
\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}
is the desired map.

Uniqueness follows because the group
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{n}(F)
is generated by all symbols of the form
\eta^{m}\cdot[u_{1}]\cdot\ldots\cdot[u_{n}]
and
\eta^{m}[\pi]\cdot[u_{2}]\cdot\ldots\cdot[u_{n}]
.
∎

For Milnor-Witt K-theory, unlike Milnor K-theory, this residue homomorphism depends not only on
\nu
but also on the choice of
\pi
.
To remove this dependency we will introduce twisted Milnor-Witt K-theory.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated field extension over
k
and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
F
.
Denote by
\mathcal{L}^{0}
the complement of the zero section of
\mathcal{L}
.
Define

K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L})\coloneqq K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[F^{\times}]}\mathbb{Z}[\mathcal{L}^{0}]\,.

The analogous definition can be made for Milnor K-theory and this allows to define twisted versions of the hyperbolic and reduction map:

h_{\mathcal{L}}\colon K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L}),\quad\rho_{\mathcal{L}}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L})

Recall that a line bundle over
F
is just a
1
-dimensional vector space, and its zero section is the zero element.
For any
l\in\mathcal{L}^{0}
there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}\cong F
descending to
\mathcal{L}^{0}\cong F^{\times}
and ultimately

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}a\otimes l

but there is no canonical choice for this.
Twisted Milnor-Witt K-theory is a priori not a ring, but only a graded
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)
-module.

Now for a valuation
\nu\colon F\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
and a uniformizing parameter
\pi
define a twisted residue homomorphism:

\displaystyle\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(\nu),(\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}^{2})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\kappa(\nu)})

\displaystyle a\otimes l

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}(a)\otimes\overline{\pi}^{\vee}\otimes l

Here
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa(\nu)}
denotes the restriction of
\mathcal{L}
to
\kappa(\nu)
,
\overline{\pi}
denotes the class of
\pi
in
\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}^{2}
, and
\overline{\pi}^{\vee}
its
\kappa(\nu)
-dual.
The appearance of
(\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu})^{\vee}
will be explained in a moment.
This homomorphism is independent of the choice of
\pi
:
For any
\pi^{\prime}\coloneqq u\cdot\pi
, we have
(\overline{\pi}^{\prime})^{\vee}=\langle u^{-1}\rangle\overline{\pi}^{\vee}
and further
\partial_{\nu}^{\pi^{\prime}}(a)=\langle u^{-1}\rangle\partial_{\nu}^{\pi}(a)
by
[
Fas20
, Remark 1.9]
, therefore we compute

\partial^{\pi^{\prime}}_{\nu}(a)\otimes(\overline{\pi}^{\prime})^{\vee}=\langle u^{-1}\rangle\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}(a)\otimes\langle u\rangle\overline{\pi}^{\vee}=\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}(a)\otimes\overline{\pi}^{\vee}\,.

Thus one can drop
\pi
from the notation.
Note that this is still dependent on the choice of
\nu
.

For the construction of Chow-Witt groups we will twist by a line bundle of the following form.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
be a morphism of schemes, with
f^{\sharp}\colon f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{O}_{X}
the associated map of sheaves.

1.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module.
A
Y
-derivation of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
into
\mathcal{E}
is a map
D\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{E}
of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-modules such that
D\circ f^{\sharp}=0
and
D
satisfies the Leibniz rule

D(ab)=aD(b)+D(a)b\,.

2.

The sheaf (or module) of relative differentials
\Omega_{X/Y}
is the
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module representing the functor
\operatorname{Der}_{f^{\sharp}}(\mathcal{O}_{X},-)
,
equipped with the universal derivation
d\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Omega_{X/Y}
.

3.

The determinant
\det\mathcal{E}
of a rank
r

\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module is defined as the highest exterior power
\Lambda^{r}\mathcal{E}
.
The determinant of
\Omega_{X/Y}
is sometimes denoted
\omega_{X/Y}
.

The existence of
\Omega_{X/Y}
is shown for example in
[
Har77
, II.8]
.
If
f
is smooth,
\Omega_{X/Y}
is locally free and is also referred to as the cotangent bundle of
f
.

Beispiel 2.11
.

1.

The sheaf of differentials
\Omega_{X/X}
along the identity on
X
is the zero sheaf.

2.

For a projective space
\mathbb{P}_{k}^{r}
, the sheaf
\omega_{\mathbb{P}^{r}/k}=\det\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{r}/k}
is isomorphic to the twisting sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(-r-1)

[
Har77
, II.8.20.1]
.

We will consider the Milnor-Witt K-theory of
\kappa(W)
twisted by the line bundle
\det(\Omega_{\kappa(W)/k})
.
If
i\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
is a regular embedding of smooth schemes, there is an isomorphism

\displaystyle\det(\Omega_{X/k})

\displaystyle\cong i^{*}\det(\Omega_{Y/k})\otimes\det(N_{X}Y)

of line bundles over
X
, where
N_{X}Y
is the normal bundle of
X
in
Y
(see after
[
Fas20
, Eq. 1.4]
).
For a discrete valuation
\nu\colon F\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}
, maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}
and residue field
\kappa(\nu)
, the quotient map
\mathcal{O}_{\nu}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\kappa(\nu)
induces a map
F=\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{O}_{\nu})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\kappa(\nu)
with kernel
\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}
, exhibiting
(\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}^{2})^{\vee}
as the normal bundle of the morphism of schemes
\Spec(\kappa(\nu))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Spec(F)
.
Therefore the above isomorphism reads

\displaystyle\det(\Omega_{\kappa(\nu)/k})

\displaystyle\cong F\otimes\det(\Omega_{F/k})\otimes(\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}^{2})^{\vee}

(1)

in this case.
This also explains the appearance of
(\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}/\mathfrak{m}_{\nu}^{2})^{\vee}
in the construction of the twisted residue homomorphism - this homomorphism can also be interpreted as

\partial^{\pi}_{\nu}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F,\det\Omega_{F/k}\otimes\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(\nu),\det\Omega_{\kappa(\nu)/k}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\kappa(\nu)})\,.

Next we will construct the so-called transfer morphism which will form the foundation for both the Gersten-Witt differential and pushforwards.
Consider the polynomial ring
F[t]
and a monic irreducible polynomial
p\in F[t]
and denote
F(p)=F[t]/(p)
.
Then the
p
-adic valuation
\nu_{p}\colon F(t)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
determines a residue homomorphism

\displaystyle\partial_{p}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F(t),\det(\Omega_{F(t)/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(F(p),(\mathfrak{m}_{p}/\mathfrak{m}_{p}^{2})^{\vee}\otimes_{F[t]}\det(\Omega_{F[t]/k}))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(F(p),\det(\Omega_{F(p)/k}))\,.

Fasel
[
Fas20
, 1.20]
proves the following, based on
[
Mor12
, 3.24]
.

Proposition 2.12
.

The sequence

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(F,\det(\Omega_{F/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(F(t),\det(\Omega_{F(t)/k}))\\
\xrightarrow{\sum_{p}\partial_{p}}\bigoplus_{p}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1}(F(p),\det(\Omega_{F(p)/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

where the sum in the third term goes over all monic irreducible polynomials in
F[t]
,
is split exact.

Denote by
\partial_{\infty}
the residue homomorphism determined by the discrete valuation

\displaystyle\nu_{\infty}\colon F(t)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}

\displaystyle f/g

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\deg(g)-\deg(f)\,.

Definition 2.13
.

1.

For a finite field extension
F/k
and a monic irreducible polynomial
p\in F[t]
define the transfer morphism as the composition

\transfer_{F(p),F}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1}(F(p),\det(\Omega_{F(p)/k}))\subseteq\bigoplus_{p}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1}(F(p),\det(\Omega_{F(p)/k}))\\
\xrightarrow{s}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(F(t),\det(\Omega_{F(t)/k}))\xrightarrow{\partial_{\infty}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1}(F,\det(\Omega_{F/k}))

where
s
is a section of
\sum_{p}\partial_{p}
as described in the previous proposition.

2.

If
L/F
is another finite field extension, choose a filtration

F=F_{0}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq F_{n}=L

such that for all
i
the field extension
F_{i}
is of the form
F_{i-1}(p_{i})
for some monic irreducible polynomial
p_{i}\in F_{i-1}[t]
, and set

\transfer_{L,F}=\transfer_{F_{1},F_{0}}\circ\ldots\circ\transfer_{F_{n},F_{n-1}}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(L,\det(\Omega_{L/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(F,\det(\Omega_{F/k}))\,.

The first part of the definition is independent of the choice of section
s
because the composite

K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(F,\det\Omega_{F/k})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(F(p),\det\Omega_{F(p)/k})\xrightarrow{\partial_{\infty}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1}(F,\det\Omega_{F/k})

is trivial as explained in
[
Fas20
, Remark 1.14]
.
The second part is proved by
[
Mor12
, 4.27]
to be independent of the chosen filtration.

Now let
X
be a (finite type, separated) scheme over
k
.
We want to construct a residue homomorphism
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i+1}(\kappa(x),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(\kappa(y),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(y)/k}))
for fixed
x\in X_{(i)}
,
y\in X_{(i+1)}
.
If
x\notin\overline{\{y\}}
set the residue homomorphism to be zero.
Otherwise denote by
\widetilde{Z}
the normalization of
\overline{\{y\}}
with all points of codimension
\geq 2
removed.
Normal implies regular and therefore, since
k
is perfect,
\widetilde{Z}
is smooth.
The morphism
i\colon\widetilde{Z}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
is finite.
Under these conditions, for any point
z\in\widetilde{Z}
of codimension
1
lying over
y
the composition

\nu_{\operatorname{div}}\colon\kappa(x)^{\times}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{div}}\mathbb{Z}\langle\overline{\{z^{\prime}\}}\mid z^{\prime}\in\widetilde{Z}^{(1)},\,i(z^{\prime})=y\rangle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}\langle z\rangle

is a discrete valuation with residue field
\kappa(z)
(see
[
Har77
, before Lemma 6.1]
) and we consider the associated boundary morphism

\partial_{\operatorname{div}}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(\kappa(x),\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(z),(\mathfrak{m}_{\operatorname{div}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\operatorname{div}}^{2})^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\widetilde{Z}})\,.

Set
\mathcal{L}=\det\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k}
and insert the isomorphism
Eq.

1
to obtain

\partial_{\operatorname{div}}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(\kappa(x),\det\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(z),\det\Omega_{\kappa(z)/k})\,.

Further if
i(z)=y
the field extension
\kappa(y)/\kappa(z)
is finite and thus there is a canonical transfer

\transfer_{\kappa(z),\kappa(y)}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(\kappa(z),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(z)/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i}(\kappa(y),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(y)/k}))\,.

Summing over the composition of these two maps for all
z\in\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}
with
i(z)=y
finally yields a boundary morphism

\partial_{x,y}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(\kappa(x),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*-1}(\kappa(y),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(y)/k}))

for the Gersten-Witt complex.
Morel
[
Mor12
, 5.31]
proves that this is in fact a chain differential under the assumption that the characteristic of
k
is coprime to
2
,
and
[
Fel20
]
removes this assumption.
With this we can finally make the following definition.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
X
be a scheme over
k
and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
X
.
For every integer
j
define the homological Gersten-Witt complex by

\displaystyle C_{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(i)}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j+i}(\kappa(x),\mathcal{L}\otimes\det(\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k}))

with differential
\partial\colon C_{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C_{i-1}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})
given by summing over the residue homomorphisms defined above.
If further
X
is smooth of dimension
d
, define the cohomological Gersten-Witt complex

\displaystyle C^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=C_{d-i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j-d,\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{X/k}^{\vee})=

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(i)}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j-i}(\kappa(x),\mathcal{L}\otimes\det(\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k})\otimes\det\Omega_{X/k}^{\vee})

with differential
\partial\colon C^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C^{i+1}(C,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})
.

The additional twist
\det\Omega_{X/k}^{\vee}
will later ensure that the pullback along a flat morphism of schemes induces no change of twist on cohomological Chow-Witt groups.
This makes cohomological Chow-Witt groups with fixed twist a contravariant functor and will further lead to the ring structure being
\Pic(X)/2)
-graded.
In contrast, for homological Chow-Witt groups the definition is chosen so that pushforward induces no change of twist.

Definition 2.15
.

Let
X
be a scheme over
k
and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
X
.
The homological respectively (if
X
is smooth) cohomological Chow-Witt groups twisted by
\mathcal{L}
are defined by

\displaystyle\chowwitt_{i}(X,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=H_{i}(C_{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},-i,\mathcal{L})_{*})

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=H^{i}(C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},i,\mathcal{L}))\,.

The Milnor-Witt cohomology groups are defined by

\displaystyle H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=H^{i}(C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L}))\,.

Spelled out, for homological Chow-Witt groups we take homology at the left-hand term of

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\bigoplus_{x\in X_{(i)}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{0}(\kappa(x),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\bigoplus_{y\in X_{(i-1)}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1}(\kappa(y),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(y)/k}))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

and for cohomological Chow-Witt groups we take cohomology at the left-hand term of

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(i)}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{0}(\kappa(x),\det(\mathfrak{m}_{x}/\mathfrak{m}_{x}^{2})^{\vee})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\bigoplus_{y\in X^{(i+1)}}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1}(\kappa(y),\det(\mathfrak{m}_{y}/\mathfrak{m}_{y}^{2})^{\vee})\ldots

i.e. we always take (co)homology at the term containing subschemes
V
of (co)dimension
i
and
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{0}
,
and in both complexes the differentials decrease dimension (=increase codimension) of subschemes and decrease the degree of
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}
.
Note that Chow-Witt groups are precisely Milnor-Witt cohomology groups in diagonal bidegrees, i.e.
i=j
.

Bemerkung 2.16
.

This is not the original definition;

[
Fas05
]
and
[
Fas08
]
originally introduced the Gersten-Witt complex as a fiber product of complexes which we will present in the next section.
The construction of the complex as detailed above is due to
[
Mor12
]
and shown by
[
AF16
, 2.8]
to be equivalent to the classical definition.

3
Fiber Product Decomposition

In this section we will introduce some more cohomology theories and their relation to Chow-Witt groups respectively Milnor-Witt cohomology.
This will not only provide context for Chow-Witt groups but also some useful tools for later computations.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated field extension over
k
.
The Witt ring
\witt(F)
consists of isometry classes of quadratic forms over
F
modulo metabolic forms (direct sum of several copies of the hyperbolic form).
It becomes a commutative ring with direct sum and tensor product.

It is immediate from this construction that the Witt ring is isomorphic to
\GW(F)/h
.

Beispiel 2.18
.

1.

The Witt ring of a quadratically closed field is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2

[
Lam05
, 3.1]
.

2.

The Witt ring of
\mathbb{R}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}

[
Lam05
, 3.2]
.

Definition 2.19
.

Let
F
be a finitely generated field extension over
k
.
Consider the rank map
\rank\colon\GW(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
.
It descends to a map
\witt(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}/\rank(h)=\mathbb{Z}/2
which is also called rank.

1.

The kernel of the latter is called the fundamental ideal
I(F)
of the Witt ring.

2.

Denote by
I^{i}(F)
the powers of this ideal and by
\overline{I}^{i}(F)
the quotient
I^{i}(F)/I^{i+1}(F)
.

3.

The Witt K-theory of
F
denoted
I^{*}(F)
(or sometimes
K^{\mathrm{W}}_{*}(F)
) is the graded ring given by
K^{\mathrm{W}}_{i}(F)=I^{i}(F)
, where for
i\leq 0
we set
I^{i}(F)=\witt(F)
.

4.

The quotient groups
\overline{I}^{i}(F)
also assemble into a graded ring
\overline{I}^{*}(F)
, sometimes called reduced Witt K-theory.

All of these have twisted variants
\witt(F,\mathcal{L})
,
I(F,\mathcal{L})
,
I^{*}(F,\mathcal{L})
,
\overline{I}^{*}(F,\mathcal{L})
for a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
over
F
.

It follows from this definition that

\witt(F)/I(F)\cong(\GW(F)/h)/I(F)\cong(\GW(F)/I(F))/h\cong\mathbb{Z}/\rank(h)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\,.

Thus one can form the following commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GW(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\witt(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(k)/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rank}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rank}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Thus the kernels of the two rank maps are isomorphic,
which justifies calling the kernel of
\rank\colon\GW(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{Z}
the fundamental ideal of the Grothendieck-Witt ring and denoting it by
I(F)
as well.

Beispiel 2.20
.

1.

If
F
is a quadratically closed field (for example
\mathbb{C}
), then
\GW(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}
and
\witt(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
.
Both rank maps are the identity and the fundamental ideal is zero.

2.

If
F=\mathbb{R}
, then
\GW(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
and
\witt(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}
.
The top rank map sends both elements
\langle 1\rangle
,
\langle-1\rangle
of
\mathbb{Z}/2
to
1
and the fundamental ideal is thus isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}
and generated by the element
\langle 1\rangle-\langle-1\rangle
.
The left vertical map sends
\langle 1\rangle
to
1
and
\langle-1\rangle
to
-1\in\witt(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and the bottom rank map is just the quotient map.

The Milnor conjecture on quadratic forms proved by
[
OVV07
, 4.1]
asserts that
\overline{I}^{*}(F)
is isomorphic to
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)/2
as graded ring.
Hence one can even form a commutative square of graded rings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I^{*}(F,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)/2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}}

which by
[
Mor04
, 5.3]
is a pullback square.
This is a fact that, to the author’s best knowledge, actually relies on the characteristic of the base field
k
being different from
2
, compare also
[
Fas20
, Rem. 1.5]
.

The inclusions
I^{i}(F,\mathcal{L})\subseteq I^{i-1}(F,\mathcal{L})
are given by multiplication with
\eta\in K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1}(F)
,
which is compatible with the differential of the Gersten-Witt complex by construction as in
Theorem

2.8
.
Therefore one can define a subcomplex

\displaystyle C^{i}(X,I^{*},j,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(i)}}I^{j+i}(\kappa(x),\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{\kappa(x)}\otimes\det\Omega_{X/k}^{\vee})

and a quotient complex

\displaystyle C^{i}(X,\overline{I}^{*},j)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(i)}}\overline{I}^{j+i}(\kappa(x))\,.

Similarly, dividing out
\eta
is compatible with the Gersten-Witt differential and thus one an define a quotient complex

\displaystyle C^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*},j)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{x\in X^{(i)}}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{i+j}(\kappa(x))\,.

Observe that the Gersten complex and cohomology with coefficients in
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)
is independent of twist, thus those for
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)/2
and
\overline{I}^{*}
are as well and therefore the line bundle
\mathcal{L}
is omitted from notation in these cases.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme.
Define its
i
-th
I^{j}
-cohomology

\displaystyle H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle=H^{i}(C^{*}(X,I^{*},j,\mathcal{L})

and reduced
I^{j}
-cohomology

\displaystyle H^{i}(X,\overline{I}^{j})

\displaystyle=H^{i}(C^{*}(X,\overline{I}^{*},j))

The groups
H^{i}(X,I^{0},\mathcal{L})
are sometimes also called Witt cohomology and denoted
H^{i}(X,\witt,\mathcal{L})
, in view of
I^{0}(F)=\witt(F)
.
Further define the Milnor cohomology and (cohomological) Chow groups of
X

\displaystyle H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})

\displaystyle=H^{i}(C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*},j))

\displaystyle\chow^{i}(X)

\displaystyle=H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{i})\,.

According to
[
AF16
, 2.8]
there is in fact a pullback square of chain complexes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*},j)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(X,I^{*},j,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{*}(X,\overline{I}^{*},j)}

Further one can deduce the following short exact sequences of graded rings from the definitions of
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}
,
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}
,
I^{*}
and
\overline{I}^{*}
, and analogous sequences of graded modules for their twisted versions.

\displaystyle 0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)\xrightarrow{h}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}I^{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

\displaystyle 0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)\xrightarrow{\cdot 2}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)/2\cong\overline{I}^{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

\displaystyle 0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}I^{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(F)\xrightarrow{\rho}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

\displaystyle 0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}I^{*+1}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}I^{*}(F)\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\rho}}\overline{I}^{*+1}(F)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

All of these induce short exact sequences on chain complexes and thus long exact cohomology sequences which assemble into the
“key diagram”
of
[
HW19
, Section 2.4]
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chow^{i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chow^{i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,I^{i+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chow^{i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(X,I^{i+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,I^{i+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,I^{i},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowmodtwo^{i}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(X,I^{i+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{mod}h_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{mod}2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bockstein}

(2)

Here
\chowmodtwo
denotes mod
2
-Chow groups.
The maps
\rho
and
\widetilde{\rho}
are both called reduction map, and
h_{\mathcal{L}}
is called the hyperbolic map.
The differential
\bockstein
is referred to as the Bockstein.
The lower horizontal sequence consisting of the maps
\bockstein
,
\eta
,
\widetilde{\rho}
sometimes gets the nickname Bär sequence.
The fact that the leftmost and rightmost vertical as well as the topmost horizontal arrows are identity maps is due to the pullback square of chain complexes above.
The four maps in the central square are ring homomorphisms.

Hornbostel-Wendt prove the following useful statement about this diagram.
This already mentions the ring structure on Chow-Witt and
I^{j}
-cohomology, which we will discuss in more detail in
Section

6
.

Proposition 2.22

(
[
HW19
, Prop. 2.11]
)
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
.
Consider the canonical ring homomorphism

c\colon\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(X)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(X)/2}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{*}(X,I^{*},-)\times_{\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}}\chowmodtwo^{*}(X)}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(X)/2}\ker\partial_{\mathcal{L}}

with structure maps for the fiber product as in the central square of the
“key diagram”
above.
This morphism is always surjective.
It is injective in a given degree
(i,\mathcal{L})
if one of the following conditions holds:

1.

\chow^{i}(X)
has no non-trivial
2
-torsion.

2.

the map
\eta\colon H^{i}(X,I^{i+1},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(X,I^{i},\mathcal{L})
is injective.

The map
c
is in fact a morphism of
\GW(k)
-algebras, where on the right-hand side
\GW(k)
acts on the first factor via the action of
\witt(k)\cong\GW(k)/h
and on the second via
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(k)\cong\GW(k)/I(k)
.

4
Functoriality: Flat Pullback and Proper Pushforward

The first goal of this section is to define for every proper morphism
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
of schemes and a line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
Y
a map
f_{*}\colon\chowwitt_{i}(X,f^{*}\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt_{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})
on Chow-Witt groups, following the exposition of
[
Fas20
, Section 2.3]
(originally due to
[
Fas08
]
).

Let
X
,
Y
be schemes over
k
,
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
Y
.
Let
x\in X_{(i)}
and
y=f(x)\in Y_{(j)}
.
We define a map

(f_{*})_{x,y}\colon K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(\kappa(x),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(x)/k})\otimes\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*+j-i}(\kappa(y),\det(\Omega_{\kappa(y)/k})\otimes\mathcal{L})

by setting
(f_{*})_{x,y}=0
if the field extension
\kappa(x)\subseteq\kappa(y)
is infinite, and
(f_{*})_{x,y}=\transfer_{\kappa(x),\kappa(y)}
if that field extension is finite and thus
i=j
.

Recall that a morphism
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
is called proper if it is separated, of finite type, and universally closed.

Satz 2.23

(Proper Pushforward (Fasel))
.

Let
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
be a proper morphism and denote by
c
the codimension of its image in
Y
.
Then the morphisms

\displaystyle f_{*}\colon C_{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C_{*}(Y,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})

and, if
X
and
Y
are smooth,

\displaystyle f_{*}\colon C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,f^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{X/k})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C^{*+c}(Y,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j+c,\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{Y/k})

obtained by summing over the
(f_{*})_{x,y}
defined above are both morphisms of complexes.
The resulting maps on Chow-Witt groups and Milnor-Witt cohomology are also denoted
f_{*}
.

Proof.

See
[
Fas08
, Corollaire 10.4.5]
.
∎

As the pushforward morphism is constructed in
[
Fas08
, Corollaire 10.4.5]
from the fiber product of chain complexes of
[
AF16
, 2.8]
(see before
Proposition

2.22
), it is evidently compatible with the structure maps
\rho
,
\modulo h
,
\widetilde{\rho}
and
\modulo 2
of this fiber product as well as the hyperbolic map
h_{\mathcal{L}}
which on the fiber product can be represented as
\cdot(0,2)
.

There is an isomorphism of line bundles
\det f^{*}\Omega_{Y/k}^{\vee}\otimes\det\Omega_{X/k}\cong\det\Omega_{X/Y}
by
[
Har77
, Prop. II.8.11]
.
Hence substituting
\mathcal{L}
for
\det\Omega_{Y/k}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{L}
allows to write the pushforward map as

\chowwitt^{i-c}(X,\det\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes f^{*}\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})\,.

(3)

For pullback morphisms there are the following two statements.

Satz 2.24

(Flat Pullback (Fasel))
.

Let
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
be a flat morphism and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
X
.
Then there are morphisms of complexes

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon C_{*}(Y,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C_{*-\codim_{Y}X}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j-\codim_{Y}X,\det\Omega_{X/Y}\otimes\mathcal{L})

and, if
X
and
Y
are smooth,

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon C^{*}(Y,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,\mathcal{L})\,.

The resulting maps on Chow-Witt groups and Milnor-Witt cohomology are denoted
f^{*}
.

Proof sketch.

This is a very vague sketch.
See
[
Fas08
, Corollaire 10.4.2]
for details.

A morphism of schemes
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
induces a functor
f^{*}\colon D^{b}(\mathcal{P}(Y))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}D^{b}(\mathcal{P}(X))
between the derived categories of bounded presheaves over
Y
and
X

and that induces a morphism on Witt groups (which we will not introduce in detail in this work).
Checking that this morphism is compatible with the inclusions of the fundamental ideals as well as the differentials of the Gersten-Witt complex produces two morphisms of complexes

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon C_{*}(Y,I^{*},j,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C_{*-\codim_{Y}X}(X,I^{*},j-\codim_{Y}X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\Omega_{X/Y})

and, if
X
and
Y
are smooth,

\displaystyle f^{*}\colon C^{*}(Y,I^{*},j,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}C^{*}(X,I^{*},j,\mathcal{L})\,.

Forming the fiber product with the Gersten complex for Milnor K-theory, after checking compatibility of
f^{*}
with the structure maps of this fiber product, yields the statement.
∎

Again, the pullback morphism is compatible with the structure maps
\rho
and
\modulo h
of this fiber product as well as the hyperbolic map
h_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Proposition 2.25

(General Pullback (Fasel))
.

Let
X
and
Y
be smooth schemes over
k
,
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
Y
and
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
any morphism of schemes.
Then there is a pullback map

f^{*}\colon\chowwitt^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i}(X,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

Proof.

See
[
Fas20
, 3.3]
.
∎

5
Properties

The following will make repeated appearance in many computations throughout this work.

Proposition 2.26

(Localization Sequence)
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
,
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
X
,
\iota\colon Z\hookrightarrow X
a closed smooth subscheme of codimension
c
and
j\colon U\hookrightarrow X
its complement.
Then there is for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
a long exact sequence called the localization sequence:

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i-c}(Z,\iota^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{Z/X})\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{j^{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(U,j^{*}\mathcal{L})\\
\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i-c+1}(Z,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-\codim_{X}(Z)},\iota^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{Z/X})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

This sequence continues indefinitely to the left and right meaning that Milnor-Witt cohomology groups of all possible bidegrees occur.

Localization sequences are compatible with pullbacks of scheme morphisms in the following sense.

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
be a morphism of smooth schemes and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle on
Y
.
Let
\iota\colon Z\subseteq Y
a closed subscheme of codimension
c
and
j\colon U=Y\smallsetminus Z\subseteq Y
its complement, and denote
X_{Z}=f^{-1}(Z)
and
X_{U}=f^{-1}(U)
.
Assume that
X_{Z}
is smooth and further that there is an isomorphism of bundles
f^{*}N_{Z}Y\cong N_{X_{Z}}X
.
Then there is a commutative ladder of localization sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i-c}(Z,\iota^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{Z/Y})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(U,j^{*}\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i-c}(X_{Z},f^{*}\iota^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{X_{Z}/X})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(X,f^{*}\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(X_{U},f^{*}j^{*}\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

Proof.

The argument for Witt cohomology from
[
HMW24
, Lemma 3.5]
translates directly to this setting.
∎

Proof.

Fasel
[
Fas20
, Section 2.2]
proves that under these conditions, there is a long exact sequence

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i-\codim_{X}(Z)}(Z,i^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det i^{*}\Omega_{X/k}^{\vee}\otimes\det\Omega_{Z/k})\xrightarrow{i_{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{j^{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(U,j^{*}\mathcal{L})\ldots

The pushforward
(s_{0})_{*}
exists because
s_{0}
is a closed immersion and thus proper.
Substituting line bundles as in
Eq.

3
yields the statement.
∎

Proposition 2.28

(Square Periodicity)
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
and
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{M}
line bundles over
X
.
For all
i
there is a canonical isomorphism

\sqperiod_{\mathcal{M}}\colon\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})\,.

When the line bundle
\mathcal{M}
is clear from context, we will sometimes drop it from the notation and simply write
\sqperiod
.

This will be crucial for defining a total Chow-Witt ring in the next section.
The statement seems to be well known, but nevertheless the author was not able to find a written account for Chow-Witt groups, so we will give a short argument here.

Proof.

Consider again the pullback square of
Section

3
.
The complexes with coefficients in
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(k)
and
\overline{I}^{*}(k)\cong K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(k)/2
are independent of twist by construction.
For the complex with coefficients in
I^{*}(k)
, the square periodicity map comes from multiplication with the quadratic form
[\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\mathcal{O}_{X}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}]\in H^{0}(X,I^{0},\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})=W^{0}(X,\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})
.
A proof that this is indeed an isomorphism can be found for example in
[
BC12
]
.
∎

From this constructions the following is immediate.

Lemma 2.29
.

The following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chow^{i}(X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqperiod}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}

Proposition 2.30

(Homotopy Invariance)
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme and
\pi\colon E\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
a vector bundle.
Then the pullback

\pi^{*}\colon\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(E)

is a ring isomorphism.

Proof.

See
[
Fas08
, Corollaire 11.3.2]
.
∎

6
Ring Structure

The first step towards defining the multiplicative structure for the Chow-Witt ring is the exterior product

\mu\colon H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})\otimes H^{i^{\prime}}(Y,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i+i^{\prime}}(X\times Y,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j+j^{\prime}},\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{L}^{\prime})

for
X
and
Y
smooth schemes and
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
line bundles over
X
and
Y
, respectively.
For this consider the fiber product of chain complexes from
Section

3
.
The external products on
C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*},j)
as defined by
[
Ros96
]
and
C^{*}(X,I^{*},j,-)
as defined by
[
GN03
]
induce a product on their fiber product
C^{*}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*},j,-)
.
Fasel
[
Fas07
, 4.12]
proves that this product is well-defined and descends to a product on cohomology.

Definition 2.31
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
and
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
line bundles over
X
.
Define the product on Milnor-Witt cohomology

H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})\otimes H^{i^{\prime}}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j^{\prime}},\mathcal{M})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i+i^{\prime}}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j+j^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})

as the composition of the exterior product
\mu
with the pullback along the diagonal map
\Delta\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X\times X
.

This product is associative by
[
Fas07
, Prop. 6.6]
.
It is neither commutative nor anti-commutative, but satisfies the following property due to
[
HW19
, Prop. 2.5]
.

Lemma 2.32
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
and
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{M}
line bundles over
X
.
Let
\alpha\in\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})
and
\beta\in\chowwitt^{j}(X,\mathcal{M})
.
Then

\beta\alpha=\langle-1\rangle^{ij}\alpha\beta\,.

It immediately follows that all classes in degree
0
are central in the Chow-Witt ring.

In
Proposition

2.28
we found that Chow-Witt groups are invariant under squares of line bundles.
So when adding up all Chow-Witt groups to form a ring we don’t want to include all of these isomorphic groups, but only sum over
\Pic(X)/2
.
For this we need to ensure the following compatibility.

Lemma 2.33
.

The square periodicity isomorphism is compatible with multiplication in the sense that the following diagram commutes for all line bundles
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
on
X
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\times\chowwitt^{j}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i+j}(X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})\times\chowwitt^{j}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\otimes(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i+j}(X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\otimes(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{mult}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqperiod_{\mathcal{M}}\times\sqperiod_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqperiod_{\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{mult}}

Proof.

As explained in the proof of
Proposition

2.28
, considering
K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}
as the fiber product
I^{*}\times_{K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}/2}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}
exhibits the square periodicity isomorphism as the fiber product of the identity on Chow groups and the square periodicity isomorphism for
I^{j}
-cohomology.
The latter is given by multiplication with the quadratic form
[\mathcal{M}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{M}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}]\in H^{0}(X,I^{0},\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})
by
[
BC12
]
.
To prove the
Lemma
we have to show that for
\alpha\in\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})
and
\beta\in\chowwitt^{j}(X,\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\sqperiod_{\mathcal{M}}(\alpha)\cdot\sqperiod_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\beta)

\displaystyle=\alpha\cdot[\mathcal{M}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{M}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}]\cdot\beta\cdot[\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}]

\displaystyle=\alpha\beta\cdot[\mathcal{M}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{M}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}]\cdot[\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}]

\displaystyle=\alpha\beta\cdot[\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\vee}\otimes(\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime})^{\otimes 2}]

\displaystyle=\sqperiod_{\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}(\alpha\beta)

The second equality holds because the class
[\mathcal{M}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{M}^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}]
lives in degree
0
and is thus in the center of the Witt cohomology ring as per
Lemma

2.32
.
The third equality follows because the product in the Witt ring
\witt(k)
is given by tensor product of quadratic forms.
∎

A very detailed account of all technicalities concerning the choices of line bundles representing classes in
\Pic(X)/2
and the necessary compatibilities can be found in
[
BC12
]
.

Definition 2.34
.

We define the total Chow-Witt ring of a smooth scheme
X
as the graded ring

\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(X)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(X)/2}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})

with the multiplication detailed above.

We will use the notation
\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
for the
\mathbb{Z}\times(\Pic(X)/2)
-graded ring, as opposed to
\chowwitt^{*}
which in the literature is commonly used to denote the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring of Chow-Witt groups with trivial twist.
The computations in this work are all concerned with the total Chow-Witt ring
\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
.

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
X
,
Y
be smooth schemes over
k
and
f\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}Y
a morphism.
Then the pullback map

f^{*}\colon\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(Y)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(X)

is a ring homomorphism.

Proof.

Previously in
Proposition

2.25
we have constructed a pullback homomorphism of groups

f^{*}\colon\chowwitt^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i}(X,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

for each
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
Y
.
These assemble into a map

\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(Y)}\chowwitt^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(Y)}\chowwitt^{i}(X,f^{*}\mathcal{L})

which is shown in
[
Fas07
, 7.2]
to be a ring homomorphism.
Further
f
induces a pullback map
f^{*}\colon\Pic(Y)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\Pic(X)
—which is in general neither injective nor surjective —and this in turn induces

\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(Y)}\chowwitt^{i}(X,f^{*}\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{M}\in\Pic(X)}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{M})\,.

Here the multiplicative structure on the left hand side is inherited from that of the right hand side and hence it is easy to check that this is a ring homomorphism as well.
Now to get to the total Chow-Witt rings indexed over the
\modulo 2
-Picard group, consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(Y)}\chowwitt^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{M}\in\Pic(X)}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(Y)/2}\chowwitt^{i}(Y,\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{M}\in\Pic(X)/2}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{*}}

where the vertical arrows are the quotient maps dividing out all square periodicity isomorphisms.
The bottom horizontal map
f^{*}
then emerges from the universal property of quotients and is automatically a ring homomorphism.
∎

There are also ring structures on Chow groups,
I^{j}
-cohomology and
\overline{I}^{j}
-cohomology defined in an analogous way as composition of an exterior product and the pullback along the diagonal map.
The reduction maps

\displaystyle\modulo h_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\colon\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(X,I^{i},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\colon\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chow^{i}(X)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}

\displaystyle\colon H^{i}(X,I^{i},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(X,\overline{I}^{i})

are all
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring homomorphisms with respect to these products,
essentially because they are induced by graded ring homomorphisms

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(k)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(k)/\eta=K^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}(k)

\displaystyle K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(k)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}(k)/h=I^{*}(k)

\displaystyle I^{*}(k)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}I^{*}(k)/\eta=I^{*}(k)/I^{*+1}(k)=\overline{I}^{*}(k)\,.

In the case of
\rho
and
\widetilde{\rho}
, these ring homomorphisms forget the
\Pic(X)/2
-grading on the domain and regard it only as a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring, with
i
-th degree

\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(X)/2}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chow^{i}(X)\quad\text{respectively}\>\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(X)/2}H^{i}(X,I^{i},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(X,\overline{I}^{i})\,.

Definition 2.36
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme.
We denote the Chow ring of
X
by

\displaystyle\chow^{*}(X)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\chow^{i}(X)

and the total
I^{j}
-cohomology ring by

\displaystyle H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(X,I^{*})

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(X)/2}H^{i}(X,I^{i},\mathcal{L})

with the multiplicative structures detailed above.

7
Equivariant Chow-Witt Rings

In this section we define the Chow-Witt ring
\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
