{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(BG)
of the classifying space
BG
of an algebraic group
G
following Totaro
[
Tot99
]
.
Such a classifying space always exists in Morel-Voevodsky’s
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category, but not in the category of schemes.
It is however possible to approximate
BG
by certain schemes and these suffice (and are in fact quite useful) to compute the Chow-Witt ring.

Proposition 2.37

(Totaro, Asok-Fasel)
.

Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
k
and
s
a natural number. Let
V
be a finite-dimensional faithful
G
-representation over
k
such that
G
acts freely outside a
G
-invariant closed subset
S\subseteq V
of codimension
\geq s
, and the quotient
(V\smallsetminus S)/G
exists as a scheme over
k
.
Then
\Pic((V\smallsetminus S)/G)
is independent of the choices of
V
and
S
if
s\geq 3
,
the Chow group
\chow^{i}((V\smallsetminus S)/G)
is independent of
V
and
S
,
and for a line bundle
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic((V\smallsetminus S)/G)
and
i<s-1
, the Chow-Witt group
\chowwitt^{i}((V\smallsetminus S)/G,\mathcal{L})
is independent of
V
and
S
.
We say that
(V\smallsetminus S)/G
is an approximation for
BG
in codimension
<s-1
.

In the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopy category, the colimit over a family
\{(V_{i}\smallsetminus S_{i})/G\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
as in the proposition with
\codim S_{i}\geq i
is in fact homotopy equivalent to the classifying space
B_{\mathrm{\acute{e}t}}G

[
MV99
, Prop. 4.2.6]
.
The computations in this work, however, will all take place in the category of schemes.
The above result is due to
[
Tot99
, 1.1]
for Chow groups and was observed by
[
AF16
, 3.3]
and
[
HW19
, 3.1]
to transfer to Chow-Witt groups.

Proof.

For the statement about the Picard group, note that according to
[
EH16
, 1.30]
it is isomorphic to the first Chow group
\chow^{1}((V\smallsetminus S)/G)

which is independent of
V
and
S
by
[
Tot14
, 2.5]
.

Fix a (finite-dimensional faithful) representation
V
and let
S\subseteq S^{\prime}\subseteq V
be two subsets satisfying the conditions.
Consider the decomposition

(S^{\prime}\smallsetminus S)/G\xhookrightarrow{i}(V\smallsetminus S/G)\xhookleftarrow{j}(V\smallsetminus S^{\prime})/G\,.

The subset
S/G\subseteq S^{\prime}/G
being closed, it follows from excision
[
AF16
, Lemma 2.13]
that
j^{*}
is an isomorphism on Chow-Witt groups in codimension
<s-1
.
In case
S
and
S^{\prime}
are smooth this also follows from the localization sequence

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i-r}((S^{\prime}\smallsetminus S)/G,i^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{\left((S^{\prime}\smallsetminus S)/G\right)/\left((V\smallsetminus S)/G\right)})\\
\xrightarrow{i_{*}}\chowwitt^{i}((V\smallsetminus S)/G,\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{j^{*}}\chowwitt^{i}((V\smallsetminus S^{\prime})/G,j^{*}\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

where
r
is the codimension of
(S^{\prime}\smallsetminus S)/G
in
(V\smallsetminus S)/G
, since Chow-Witt groups (and, in fact, all Milnor-Witt and similar cohomology groups) vanish in negative codimension.
For two closed subsets
S\nsubseteq S^{\prime}
apply the same argument to
S\subseteq S\cup S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}\subseteq S\cup S^{\prime}
.

Now let
V
and
W
be two representations with subsets
S\subseteq V
and
T\subseteq W
satisfying the conditions, and let
s
be a mutual lower bound for the codimensions of these subsets.
Consider the vector bundles
(V\times W)\smallsetminus(S\times W)/G\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(V\smallsetminus S)/G
and
(V\times W)\smallsetminus(V\times T)/G\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(W\smallsetminus T)/G
.
Independence of
S
as proven above shows that the two total spaces have isomorphic Chow-Witt groups in degrees
<s-1
,
and homotopy invariance shows that the same is true for the base spaces.
∎

Beispiel 2.38
.

Consider the group
\mathbb{G}_{m}
, acting by multiplication on
V=\mathbb{A}^{r+1}
for some
r\geq 1
.
This action is free outside of
S=\{0\}
which has codimension
r+1
.
The quotient
(\mathbb{A}^{r-1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong\mathbb{P}^{r}
exists as a scheme.
Thus
\mathbb{P}^{r}
is an approximation for
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
in codimension
<r
.

Fasel
[
Fas13
, 11.8]
computes

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\GW(k)&$i=0$, or $i=r$ and $r$ odd\\
\mathbb{Z}&$0<i\leq r$ and $i$ even\\
2\mathbb{Z}&$0<i<r$ and $i$ odd\end{cases}

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(1))

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}2\mathbb{Z}&$0\leq i<r$ and $i$ even\\
\mathbb{Z}&$0\leq i\leq r$ and $i$ odd\\
\GW(k)&$i=r$ and $r$ even.\end{cases}

Thus we find

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\GW(k)&$i=0$\\
\mathbb{Z}&$i>0$ and $i$ even\\
2\mathbb{Z}&$i>0$ and $i$ odd\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(1))

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}2\mathbb{Z}&$i\geq 0$ and $i$ even\\
\mathbb{Z}&$i\geq 0$ and $i$ odd\end{cases}

The factor
2
indicated that the image of the respective group under reduction
\rho
to the Chow group is generated by
2
.

Beispiel 2.39
.

Denote by
V_{a}
the line bundle over
\Spec(k)
associated to the
1
-dimension representation of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
given by
\lambda.v=\lambda^{n}\cdot v
.
Then
(V_{n}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}))/\mathbb{G}_{m}
is a line bundle over
(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong\mathbb{P}^{r}
and the complement of its zero section is an approximation for
B\mu_{n}
in codimension
<r
, as will be explained in
Section

9
.
This approximation was already used by
[
Tot14
]
and
[
Bro03
]
to compute the Chow groups of
B\mu_{n}
and then adapted by
[
LM23
]
to compute the Chow-Witt groups of
B\mu_{n}
if
n
is even.
For both computations, one can consider the localization sequence associated to the decomposition

\mathbb{P}^{r}\xrightarrow{s_{0}}(V_{n}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}))/\mathbb{G}_{m}\hookleftarrow\left(\left(V_{n}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\right)/\mathbb{G}_{m}\right)\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r})\,.

For Chow groups it then easily follows that

\chow^{i}(B\mu_{n})\cong\chow^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})/n

and one obtains

\chow^{*}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\chern]/n\cdot\chern

where
\chern
corresponds to the first Chern class of the tautological bundle
\mathcal{O}_{B\mu_{n}}(-1)
.
For Chow-Witt groups the sequence will turn out to be more complicated.
This will be the subject of
Section

10
.

The existence of such
V
and
S
for a given linear algebraic group
G
and codimension
s
is guaranteed by
[
Tot99
, Remark 1.4]
.
This allows for the following definition.

Definition 2.40
.

Let
G
be a linear algebraic group over
k
.
Set

\displaystyle\Pic(BG)

\displaystyle\cong\Pic((V\smallsetminus S)/G)

for
V
and
S
as in
Proposition

2.37
with codimension of
S
greater than
2
, and for
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(BG)
set

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(BG,\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\cong\chowwitt^{i}((V\smallsetminus S)/G,\mathcal{L})

for
V
and
S
as in
Proposition

2.37
with codimension of
S
greater than
i+1
.
The product of two classes
\alpha\in\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L})
,
\beta\in\chowwitt^{j}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{M})

is defined as their product in an approximation of
BG
in codimensions
\geq i+j
.
This is well-defined because the map
j^{*}
in the proof of
Proposition

2.37
is a ring homomorphism.
The ring obtained in this way is the total Chow-Witt ring
\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(BG)
.
The Chow ring
\chow^{*}(BG)
is defined analogously.

The product obtained this way is well-defined because the map
j^{*}
in the localization sequence in the proof of
Proposition

2.37
is a ring homomorphism.

The same argument as for
Proposition

2.37
proves the following two statements.

Korollar 2.41
.

Let
G
,
V
and
S
be as in
Proposition

2.37
.
Then for
i<s-1
and all
j
, the Milnor-Witt cohomology
H^{i}((V\smallsetminus S)/G,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})
is independent of the choice of
V
and
S
.

Korollar 2.42
.

Let
G
,
V
and
S
be as in
Proposition

2.37
.
Then for
i<s-1
and all
j
, the
I^{j}
-cohomology
H^{i}((V\smallsetminus S)/G,I^{j},\mathcal{L})
is independent of the choice of
V
and
S
.

This allows for a definition of non-diagonal Milnor-Witt and
I^{j}
-cohomology groups of a classifying space completely analogous to
Definition

2.40
.

Korollar 2.43
.

Let
G
and
H
be linear algebraic groups over
k
.
Let
V
be a finite-dimensional faithful
G
-representation with subset
S\subseteq V
of codimension
\geq s
and
W
a finite-dimensional faithful
H
-representation with subset
T\subseteq W
of codimension
\geq t
as in
Proposition

2.37
.
Then
\chowwitt^{i}((V\smallsetminus S)/G\times(W\smallsetminus T)/H,\mathcal{L})
for
i<\min(s,t)-1
is independent of
V
,
S
,
W
and
T
.

Proof.

Consider
V\times W
as the obvious representation of the group
G\times H
.
Then
G\times H
acts freely outside of
(S\times W)\cup(V\times T)
and the latter has codimension
\geq\min(s,t)
in
V\times W
.
Thus one can apply
Proposition

2.37
.
∎

8
Euler Classes

Definition 2.44
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme,
\pi\colon E\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
a vector bundle of rank
r
with zero section
s_{0}
,
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
X
and
i
an integer.
The Euler map of
\pi
is defined as the composition

\chowwitt^{i-r}(X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\omega_{E/X}))\xrightarrow{(s_{0})_{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(E,\pi^{*}\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(\pi^{*})^{-1}}\chowwitt^{i}(X,\mathcal{L})

and the Euler class
\eulercw(E)
or
\eulercw(\pi)\in\chowwitt^{r}(X,\omega_{E/X}^{\vee})
is the image of
1\in\chowwitt^{0}(X,\mathcal{O})
under this map.
The same definition can be made for
I^{j}
-cohomology (also called Euler class
\euleri
), Chow groups (called top Chern class
\chern_{r}
) and mod
2
-Chow groups (called top Stiefel-Whitney class
\sw_{r}
).

Since in this work we will only ever deal with top Chern and Stiefel-Whitney classes, we will omit the index
r
from the notation.
As explained in
[
Fas08
, Section 13.2]
there is an isomorphism of vector bundles
\det(\Omega_{E/X})\cong\pi^{*}\det(E^{\vee})
.
Note that the line bundles
\det E
and
\det E^{\vee}
differ by a square and thus the Chow-Witt groups twisted by these are isomorphic.
This justifies considering
\eulercw(E)
to live in
\chowwitt^{r}(X,\det E^{\vee})
even though technically it lives in
\chowwitt^{r}(X,\det E)
, which in some cases makes the notation slightly more readable (e.g. writing
\mathcal{O}(1)
instead of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
).
As explained after
Theorems

2.23
and
2.24
, the Euler map is compatible with the maps
\rho
,
\widetilde{\rho}
,
\modulo h_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\modulo 2
introduced in the diagram
Eq.

2
, that is,
\rho(\eulercw(E))=\chern(E)
,
\widetilde{\rho}(\euleri(E))=\sw(E)
and so on.
Further the hyperbolic map
h_{\det\Omega_{E/X}}
sends
\chern(E)
to
h\eulercw(E)
:
The hyperbolic map commutes with the Euler map, and since the latter is
\GW(k)
-linear it sends
h_{\mathcal{O}}(1)=h=h\cdot 1
to
h\cdot\eulercw(E)
.

We will prove a formula for the Euler class of the tensor product of certain line bundles, following
[
Lev20
, Theorem 10.1(2)]
.

Proposition 2.45
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme over
k
and
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{M}
line bundles over
X
.
Then

\eulercw(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})=\eulercw(\mathcal{L})+h_{\mathcal{L}}(\chern(\mathcal{M}))

where
h_{\mathcal{L}^{\vee}}\colon\chow^{1}(X)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{1}(X,\mathcal{L})
is the hyperbolic map.
In particular:

\eulercw(\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n})=\begin{cases}\frac{n}{2}\cdot h_{\mathcal{O}}(\chern(\mathcal{L}))&$n$ even\\
\frac{n}{2}h\cdot\eulercw(\mathcal{L})&$n$ odd\end{cases}

Proof.

Let us first recall where all the occurring classes live.
The Euler class
\eulercw(\mathcal{L})
is an element of
\chowwitt^{1}(X,\mathcal{L})
, and likewise
\eulercw(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})\in\chowwitt^{1}(X,\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})
which by quadratic periodicity (
Proposition

2.28
) is isomorphic to
\chowwitt^{1}(X,\mathcal{L})
.
The Chern class
\chern(\mathcal{M})
lives in
\chow^{1}(X)
.

We start by proving the statement for the classifying space for line bundles
B\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
together with the tautological bundle
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}(-1)
.

B\mathbb{G}_{m}
is not a scheme but a motivic space, but according to
Proposition

2.37
one can understand its Chow-Witt groups and Euler classes through
\mathbb{P}^{r}
for
r
sufficiently large.
Denote by
\mathcal{O}(n,m)
the line bundle over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
that is the tensor product of the pullback of
\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(n)
on the first factor and the pullback of
\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(m)
on the second factor.
We want to determine the Euler class of the bundle
\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{2}=\mathcal{O}(-1,-2)
over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
Consider the reduction map
\rho
to
\chow^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})
which maps the Euler class of a line bundle to its first Chern class.
The first Chern class is additive, thus
\rho\eulercw(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})=\chern(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})=\chern(\mathcal{L})+2\chern(\mathcal{M})
.

Under the same map
\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(-1,0))
is sent to
\chern(\mathcal{O}(0,-1))
, and
h_{\mathcal{O}(-1,0)}(\chern(\mathcal{O}(0,-1)))
is sent to
2\chern(\mathcal{O}(0,-1))
.
The reduction map is injective in this case, since its kernel is the image of
H^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},I^{1},\mathcal{O}(-1,-2))
in the long exact sequence coming from the sequence of sheaves
I^{j+1}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j}
as stated in
Section

3
, and that group will be shown to vanish in
Theorem

4.5
(which does not rely on this
Proposition
).
From this it follows that
\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,-2))=\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))+h_{\mathcal{O}(-1,0)}(\chern(\mathcal{O}(0,-1)))
.

Now for the general statement.
Let
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{M}
be two line bundles over
X
.
According to
[
MV99
, Prop. 4.3.8]
there exist classifying maps
f,g\colon X\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}B\mathbb{G}_{m}
so that
\mathcal{L}\cong f^{*}\mathcal{O}(-1)
and
\mathcal{M}\cong g^{*}\mathcal{O}(-1)
and therefore
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2}\cong(f,g)^{*}\mathcal{O}(-1,-2)
over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
Thus we compute:

\displaystyle\eulercw(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\otimes 2})

\displaystyle=\eulercw((f,g)^{*}\mathcal{O}(-1,-2))

\displaystyle=(f,g)^{*}\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,-2))

\displaystyle=(f,g)^{*}\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))+(f,g)^{*}h_{\mathcal{O}(-1,0)}(\chern(\mathcal{O}(0,-1)))

\displaystyle=(f,g)^{*}\eulercw(\mathcal{O}{-1,0})+h_{(f,g)^{*}\mathcal{O}(-1,0)}((f,g)^{*}\chern(\mathcal{O}(0,-1)))

\displaystyle=\eulercw(\mathcal{L})+h_{\mathcal{L}}(\chern(\mathcal{M}))

The statement about
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes n}
can be deduced inductively, inserting
h_{\mathcal{L}}(\chern\mathcal{L})=h\eulercw(\mathcal{L})
in the odd case.
∎

Bemerkung 2.46
.

For arbitrary line bundles
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
over a scheme
X
there is no formula of this kind as explained in the third paragraph of
[
Lev20
, Section 10]
:
Consider the universal case
X=B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
,
\mathcal{L}=\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(-1)
,
\mathcal{M}=\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(-1)
.
Then

\displaystyle\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))

\displaystyle\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1,0))

\displaystyle\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(0,-1))

\displaystyle\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(0,1))

\displaystyle\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0)\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,-1))=\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,-1))

\displaystyle\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1,1))\,.

Thus to express
\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,-1))
in terms of
\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))
and
\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(0,-1))
, one would additionally need classes in degrees
(0,\mathcal{O}(1,0))
and
(0,\mathcal{O}(0,1))
.
Levine now claims that the groups in those degrees vanish, which is not quite correct since
[
Wen24
, Theorem 1.1]
does not imply
\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1))=0
as stated in
[
Lev20
, Section 10, p.2220]
.
The rest of Levine’s argument, however, still stands: in
Corollary

5.3
we prove that the subgroup

\left(\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1,0))\cdot\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(0,1))\right)\\
+\left(\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(0,1))\cdot\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1,0))\right)\subseteq\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1,1))

does not contain
\eulercw(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})
but only
2\cdot\eulercw(\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M})
(using the symbols of
Corollary

5.3
: the subgroup on the left is generated by
H_{1}e_{2}
and
H_{2}e_{1}
, and the only relation in degree
(1,\mathcal{O}(1,1))
is
H_{1}e_{2}+H_{2}e_{1}-he_{3}
from
Eq.

9
, so
e_{3}
cannot be expressed as a linear combination of
H_{1}e_{2}
and
H_{2}e_{1}
).

Chapter 3
The Chow-Witt Ring of
B\mu_{n}

Throughout this chapter, let
n
be a positive natural number and the base field
k
be a perfect field with characteristic coprime to
2
and
n
.
Denote by
\mu_{n}
the group of roots of unity, a linear algebraic group given as a scheme by
\Spec(k[x]/(x^{n}-1))
.
The main result in this chapter is to compute the total Chow-Witt ring

\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(B\mu_{n})/2}\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{L})

for odd
n
.
The strategy closely follows that for even
n
due to
[
LM23
]
.
The model for
B\mu_{n}
described here was originally constructed in
[
Voe03
, Lemma 6.3]
and used in
[
Bro03
, Theorem 7.1]
and
[
Tot14
, Theorem 2.10]
to compute its Chow groups.

9
A Model for
B\mu_{n}

We construct an approximation of
B\mu_{n}
in the sense of
Proposition

2.37
.

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
denote by
V_{a}
the vector bundle over
\Spec(k)
associated to the
1
-dimensional representation of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
with action given by
\lambda.v=\lambda^{a}\cdot v
.
Products of such representations are always considered with diagonal action.
As explained in
Example

2.38

(V_{1}^{r}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
is an approximation of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
in codimension
<r-1
.
Over this space, consider the bundle

E_{n}^{r}\coloneqq\left(V_{n}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\right)/\mathbb{G}_{m}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}

given by projection on the second factor.

Lemma

3.2
will show that this is a line bundle.
Note that the zero coordinate in the factor
V_{n}
is the image of the zero section
s_{0}
of the line bundle
(V_{n}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}))/\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Lemma 3.1
.

There is a map of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{r}

\left(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}\right)/\mu_{n}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\left((V_{n}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\right)/\mathbb{G}_{m}

inducing isomorphisms on Chow-Witt groups in codimension
<r-1
.

Proof.

See the proof of
[
Bro03
, Theorem 7.1(i)]
.
∎

Since
(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mu_{n}
is an approximation for
B\mu_{n}
in codimension
<r-1
, this shows that
E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r})
is also an approximation for computing these Chow-Witt groups.
The advantage of using
E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r})
over
(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mu_{n}
is that the former can be embedded into a line bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{r}
, namely the twisting bundle
\mathcal{O}(n)
, as the complement of the zero section as shown in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
LM23
, Section 4.1.4]
)
.

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the line bundle
E_{n}^{r}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(n)
over
\mathbb{P}^{r}
.

Proof.

We start by proving the statement for
n=-1
.
The tautological line bundle
\mathcal{O}(-1)
is the subscheme of
\mathbb{P}^{r}\times\mathbb{A}^{r+1}
containing those points
([X_{0}:\ldots:X_{r}],(Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{r}))
for which there is a scalar
t
such that
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})=t(X_{0},\ldots,X_{r})
.
Denote by
L
the pullback of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
along
V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}V_{1}^{r+1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong\mathbb{P}^{r}
.
This is the subscheme of
(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times V_{0}^{r+1}
containing those points
((X_{0},\ldots,X_{r}),(Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{r}))
for which there is a scalar
t
such that
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{r})=t(X_{0},\ldots,X_{r})
.
This is an
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-invariant subscheme and thus inherits the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action from
(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times V_{0}^{r+1}
.

Now consider the map

\displaystyle\varphi\colon L

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times V_{-1}

\displaystyle((X_{0},\ldots,X_{r}),(Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{r}))

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{r},t)

\displaystyle=((X_{0},\ldots,X_{r}),(tX_{0},\ldots,tX_{r}))

which is readily checked to be an isomorphism.
This map is equivariant with respect to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action previously defined on each side:

\varphi(\lambda.(X,Y))=\varphi(\lambda.(X,tX))=\varphi((\lambda X,tX))=(X,t\cdot\lambda^{-1})=\lambda.\varphi((X,tX))

Therefore
(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\times V_{-1}/\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong L/\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong\mathcal{O}(-1)
.

All other line bundles
\mathcal{O}(n)
can be constructed as tensor powers of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
, adhering to the rule
\mathcal{O}(m)\otimes\mathcal{O}(n)\cong\mathcal{O}(m+n)
.
Using that tensor products commute with quotients we have

\displaystyle\left(\left(V_{m}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\right)/\mathbb{G}_{m}\right)\otimes\left(\left(V_{n}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\right)/\mathbb{G}_{m}\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\left((V_{m}\otimes V_{n})\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\right)/\mathbb{G}_{m}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\left(V_{m+n}\times(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})\right)/\mathbb{G}_{m}

as line bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{r}\cong(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Thus the statement holds for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

The scheme
E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r})
will serve as an approximation of
B\mu_{n}
in the sense of
Proposition

2.37
throughout the next sections.
The bundle map
\pi\colon E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}
induces a ring homomorphism

pi^{*}\colon\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{r})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r}))

for all
r
and thus also

\pi^{*}\colon\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})\,.

10
Computing the Chow-Witt Groups

The following known result will be used in the computation.

Satz 3.3

(
[
Fas13
, Theorem 11.7]
)
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic coprime to
2
.
Then we have the following isomorphisms of
\GW(k)
-modules.

\displaystyle H^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j}(k)&i=0\\
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j-i}(k)&$i\geq 2$ even\\
2K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j-i}(k)&$i\geq 1$ odd\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle H^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{O}(1))

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}2K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j-i}(k)&$i\geq 0$ even\\
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j-i}(k)&$i\geq 1$ odd.\\
\end{cases}

The factor
2
indicates that the image of the respective group under reduction
\rho
to the Chow group is generated by
2
.

The total Chow-Witt ring of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
has been computed in
[
Wen24
, Theorem 1.1]
:

Proposition 3.4

(Wendt)
.

There is an isomorphism of
\GW(k)
-algebras

\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\GW(k)[e,H]/(I(k)\cdot e,I(k)\cdot H,H^{2}-2h)

where
e\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1))
corresponds to the Euler class of
\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(-1)
and
H
corresponds to the element represented by
(0,2)
in

\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(1))\subseteq H^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},I^{0},\mathcal{O}(1))\times\chow^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\,.

The inclusion in the last line is in fact an inclusion by
[
HW19
, Prop. 2.11]
if
\chow^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
has trivial
2
-torsion which is satisfied since by
[
Tot14
, Thm 2.10]
,
\chow^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

By
[
EH16
, Prop. 1.30]
, the Picard group of
B\mu_{n}
is isomorphic to
\chow^{1}(B\mu_{n})
which is shown in
[
Tot14
, Theorem 2.10]
to be isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/n
.
Thus for even
n
, we need to consider two equivalence classes of line bundles in
\Pic(B\mu_{n})/2
represented by
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}(1)
(defined as the pullbacks of the respective line bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r}
), and for odd
n
only the trivial line bundle
\mathcal{O}
.

For even
n
, the Chow-Witt ring has been computed in
[
LM23
, Theorem 5.3.4]
.

Definition 3.5

(di Lorenzo-Mantovani)
.

1.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme and
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
X
.
Let
s
be a global section of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes 2}
with smooth and non-empty vanishing locus
D\subseteq X
,
and denote
U\coloneqq X\smallsetminus D
.
Then there is a non-degenerate quadratic form on
\mathcal{L}|_{U}

\displaystyle q\colon\mathcal{L}|_{U}\otimes\mathcal{L}|_{U}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{O}_{U}

\displaystyle a\otimes b

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}(a\otimes b)/s

determining an element
q_{\mathrm{gen}}\in\chowwitt^{0}(U,\mathcal{O}_{U})
.
This element satisfies
h\cdot(q_{\mathrm{gen}}-1)=0
and is mapped to
\eta\otimes\bar{f}^{\vee}
by the boundary map

\partial\colon\chowwitt^{0}(U)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{0}(D,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1},\mathcal{O}_{D})

in the localization sequence associated to the embedding
D\subseteq X
,
where
f
is a local equation with vanishing set
D
and
f^{\vee}
its dual
[
LM23
, Lemma 3.3.2]
.

2.

Let
n
be even and consider the scheme
E_{n}^{r}
constructed in the previous section with the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{E_{n}^{r}}(n/2)
.
Since
E_{n}^{r}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(n)
as line bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{r}
, the line bundle

\mathcal{O}_{E_{n}^{r}}(n/2)^{\otimes 2}\cong\mathcal{O}_{E_{n}^{r}}(n)\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}E_{n}^{r}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)\times_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)

comes equipped with a section given by the diagonal map whose vanishing locus is precisely the zero section
s_{0}
of the bundle
E_{n}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}
.
Applying the construction from the first part of this definition yields an element in the zeroth Chow-Witt group and we set

U\coloneqq q_{\mathrm{gen}}-1\in\chowwitt^{0}(E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0},\mathcal{O})\cong\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\,.

Satz 3.6

(di Lorenzo-Mantovani)
.

There are isomorphisms of groups

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\GW(k)\oplus\witt(k)&i = 0\\
\mathbb{Z}/2n&i $\geq$ 2 \text{even}\\
\mathbb{Z}/(\frac{n}{2})&i \text{odd}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(1))

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}&i = 0\\
\mathbb{Z}/(\frac{n}{2})&i $\geq$ 2 \text{even}\\
\mathbb{Z}/2n&i \text{odd.}\end{cases}

Further there is an isomorphism of
\GW(k)
-algebras

\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})\cong\GW(k)[U,H,e]/(I(k)H,I(k)e,hU,HU,nHe,H^{2}-2h,U^{2}+2U,Ue-2ne)

where
e
and
H
correspond to the pullbacks of the respective elements described in
Proposition

3.4
and
U\in\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})
is as constructed in
Definition

3.5
.

We will compute the case of odd
n
.
Following the strategy of
[
LM23
, Prop. 5.2.3]
for even
n

consider the localization sequence associated to the decomposition

\mathbb{P}^{r}\xhookrightarrow{s_{0}}E_{n}^{r}\xhookleftarrow{\iota}E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r})

which reads as follows.

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i-1}(\mathbb{P}^{r},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{E_{n}^{r}/\mathbb{P}^{r}})\xrightarrow{(s_{0})_{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(E_{n}^{r},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0},\iota^{*}\mathcal{L})\\
\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\det\Omega_{E_{n}^{r}/\mathbb{P}^{r}})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

In the first term
Lemma

3.2
implies
\det\Omega_{E_{n}^{r}/\mathbb{P}^{r}}\cong\det\Omega_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)/\mathbb{P}^{r}}
which in turn is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(n)

[
Har77
, Example 8.20.1]
and thus trivial for odd
n
.
By homotopy invariance, the pullback along the bundle map
\mathrm{pr}\colon E_{n}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}
induces an isomorphism on Chow-Witt groups.
The composition

\chowwitt^{i-1}(\mathbb{P}^{r},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}(n))\xrightarrow{(s_{0})_{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(E_{n}^{r},\mathcal{L})\xleftarrow[\cong]{\mathrm{pr}^{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\mathcal{L})

is multiplication with the Euler class of the line bundle
E_{n}^{r}
by
Definition

2.44
.
Now using that
\mathbb{P}^{r}
and
E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}
are approximations for
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
respectively
B\mu_{n}
in the sense of
Proposition

2.37
, for
r>i
this localization sequence is isomorphic to the following one.

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i-1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\eulercw(E_{n}^{r})\cdot}\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{(\iota\circ\mathrm{pr})^{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\iota^{*}\mathcal{L})\\
\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

(4)

Note that
\iota\circ\mathrm{pr}=\pi
.

Equipped with this we can compute the group structure.

Satz 3.7
.

Let
n
be odd.
Then:

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\GW(k)&i = 0\\
\mathbb{Z}/n&i $\geq$ 1\end{cases}

Proof.

For
i>0
, insert
Theorems

3.3
and
3.4
to see that the localization sequence
Eq.

4
evaluates to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phantom{H}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(k)\langle e^{i-1}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(k)\langle e^{i}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{-1}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\langle e^{i-1}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\langle e^{i}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot\eulercw(E_{n}^{r})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot(-n)e}

for
i
even and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(k)\langle He^{i-1}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2K^{\mathrm{M}}_{0}(k)\langle He^{i}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{-1}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\langle He^{i-1}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\langle He^{i}\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot\eulercw(E_{n}^{r})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot(-n)e}

for
i
odd.
Since
E_{n}^{r}\cong\mathcal{O}(n)
as line bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{r}
by
Lemma

3.2
, its Euler class evaluates to
n/2h\cdot\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(1))=-n/2he=-ne
using
Proposition

2.45
.
From this it follows that
\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/n
for
0<i\leq n
.
Denoting
\pi\colon E_{n}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}
or by abuse of notation also
\pi\colon B\mu_{n}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}B\mathbb{G}_{m}
and using the symbols from
Proposition

3.4
, one can also write

\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}/n\langle\pi^{*}(e^{i})\rangle&$0<i\leq n$, $i$ even\\
\mathbb{Z}/n\langle\pi^{*}(He^{i})\rangle&$0<i\leq n$, $i$ odd.\\
\end{cases}

(5)

For
i=0
we get
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{-1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O}(n))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1},\mathcal{O}(n))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\GW(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\mathrm{M}}_{-1}(k)=0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

which immediately implies
\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\cong\GW(k)
.
∎

11
Multiplicative Structure

For odd
n
we observed in
Theorem

3.7
that all Chow-Witt groups of
B\mu_{n}
are isomorphic to those of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
modulo
ne
.
Since the quotient map is the map
\pi^{*}
from the localization sequence
4
and this is a ring homomorphism, the Chow-Witt ring of
B\mu_{n}
is also isomorphic to that of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
modulo
n\pi^{*}(e)
and modulo the quadratic periodicity isomorphism

\psi_{1}\colon\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(1))\cong\chowwitt^{i}\left(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}\left((n+1)/2\right)^{\otimes 2}\right)\cong\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\,.

This is not true for even
n
, since in that case there is an element in
\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})
(denoted
U
in
[
LM23
]
) which is not in the image of
\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
.

Since
\sqperiod
and thus also
\psi_{1}
is compatible with multiplication (
Lemma

2.33
) it suffices to understand its action on the generators
\pi^{*}(H)
and
\pi^{*}(e)
.
Consider

\pi^{*}(H)=(0,2)\in\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(1))\subseteq H^{0}(B\mu_{n},I^{0},\mathcal{O}(1))\times_{\chowmodtwo^{0}(B\mu_{n})}\chow^{0}(B\mu_{n})

where the inclusion is justified by
Proposition

2.22
and the fact that
\chow^{0}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}
has trivial
2
-torsion.
On the second factor,
\psi_{1}
is the identity.
On the first,
\psi_{1}
being a group homomorphism already implies that it maps
0
to
0
.
Thus

\psi_{1}(\pi^{*}(H))=(0,2)\in\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\subseteq H^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\times_{\chowmodtwo^{0}(B\mu_{n})}\chow^{0}(B\mu_{n})

(6)

which under the isomorphism
\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\cong\GW(k)
corresponds to the hyperbolic form
h
.
Therefore the class
\pi^{*}(H)
is not required as a generator for the Chow-Witt ring of
B\mu_{n}
.

The relation
I(k)\cdot H
from
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
now becomes
I(k)\cdot h
which already holds in
\GW(k)
.
Similarly
H^{2}-2h
becomes
h^{2}-2h
which is also true in
\GW(k)
.
The relation
I(k)\cdot e
just becomes
I(k)\cdot\pi^{*}(e)
.

Because Euler classes are compatible with pullbacks,
\pi^{*}(e)
is the Euler class of
\mathcal{O}_{B\mu_{n}}(-1)
and we can rename it into
e
.
This yields the following concise description.

Satz 3.8
.

For
n
odd, there is an isomorphism of
\GW(k)
-algebras

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\GW(k)[e]/(I(k)\cdot e,n\cdot e)

where
e\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mu_{n})
is the Euler class of the tautological line bundle
\mathcal{O}(-1)
on
B\mu_{n}
.

To obtain this description it is not necessary to compute
\psi_{1}\pi^{*}(e)
, but for better understanding we will illustrate the argument anyway.
Consider a second isomorphism

\psi_{2}\colon\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(2))\cong\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(n+1)^{\otimes 2})\cong\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})

coming from the isomorphism of line bundles
\mathcal{O}(2)\cong\mathcal{O}(n+2)
and the square periodicity isomorphism
\sqperiod_{\mathcal{O}(n+1)}
.
This fits into the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(1))\times\chowwitt^{j}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i+1}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip 30.0pt\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(n+1))\times\chowwitt^{j}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(n+1))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i+j}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(2n+2))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\times\chowwitt^{j}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{i+j}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{mult}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{1}\times\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqperiod_{\mathcal{O}((n+1)/2)}\times\sqperiod_{\mathcal{O}((n+1)/2)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{mult}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sqperiod_{\mathcal{O}(n+1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{mult}}

The bottom square commutes by
Lemma

2.33
and the top one because the ring multiplication is constructed to be compatible with isomorphisms of line bundles.
With this one can compute:

\displaystyle\psi(\pi^{*}(e))

\displaystyle=(n+1)\cdot\psi(\pi^{*}(e))

\displaystyle=\frac{n+1}{2}h\psi(\pi^{*}(e))=\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot\psi(\pi^{*}(H))\psi(\pi^{*}(e))

\displaystyle=\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot\psi^{*}\pi^{*}(He)=\frac{n+1}{2}\cdot\pi^{*}(He)\,.

The first equality follows from the relation
n\pi^{*}(e)
, the second from the relation
I(k)\cdot e
and
\GW(k)
-linearity of
\pi^{*}
and
\psi
, and the third from
Eq.

6
.
The second to last equality comes from the above diagram
and the last one holds because
\mathcal{O}(2n+2)
is already a square over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
and thus
\psi_{2}
is already divided out in
\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
.

12
Milnor-Witt Cohomology in Non-Diagonal Bidegrees

The following will become useful in later computations.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
i,\,j
integers with
i\neq 0
.
If
j<i
then

H^{i}(B\mu_{n},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})\cong 0\,.

Proof.

Consider the localization sequence from the proof of
Theorem

3.7
.
Inserting
Theorem

3.3
shows that unless
i=0
and
\mathcal{L}
is trivial, this is isomorphic to

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j-i}(k)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j-i}(k)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(B\mu_{n},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j-i-1}(k)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

Since
K^{\mathrm{M}}_{<0}(k)=0
this implies
H^{i}(B\mu_{n},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})=0
for
j<i
.
∎

13
I^{j}
-Cohomology of
B\mu_{n}

Korollar 3.10
.

If
n
is odd:

\displaystyle H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
