\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n},I^{*})

\displaystyle\cong\witt(k)

If
n
is even:

\displaystyle H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n},I^{*})

\displaystyle\cong\witt(k)[U,e]/(I(k)e,U^{2}+2U,Ue)

Proof.

Combine
Theorems

3.6
and
3.8
and the fact that the sequence of abelian groups

\chow^{i}(B\mu_{n})\xrightarrow{h_{\mathcal{O}}}\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(B\mu_{n},I^{i},\mathcal{O})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

is exact.

First consider odd
n
.
The composition
\rho\circ h_{\mathcal{O}}
equals multiplication with
2
and further it follows from the previous computations of the groups
\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n})
and
\chow^{i}(B\mu_{n})
that the reduction map
\rho
is injective in positive degrees.
For degree zero consider the fiber product formula from
Proposition

2.22
using that
\chow^{0}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}
has no
2
-torsion.
Note that the structure map
\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{0}(B\mu_{n},I^{0},\mathcal{O})
is given by dividing out the image of
h_{\mathcal{O}}
and clearly
\modulo h_{\mathcal{O}}\circ h_{\mathcal{O}}=0
,
and recall that the pair
(0,2)
in this fiber product corresponds to the hyperbolic form
h\in\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})
.
Thus the hyperbolic map
h_{\mathcal{O}}
acts by

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{O}}\colon\chow^{*}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\chern]/(n\chern)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\GW(k)[e]/(I(k)\cdot e,ne)\cong\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}h

\displaystyle\chern^{i}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}he^{i}=2e^{i}

Since
2e^{i}
generates
\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}/n\langle e^{i}\rangle
as a
\GW(k)
-module for
i\geq 1
, this means that all higher
I^{j}
-cohomology groups vanish.
In degree
0

H^{0}(B\mu_{n},I^{0},\mathcal{O})\cong\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})/h\cong\GW(k)/h\cong\witt(k)\,.

If
n
is even an analogous argument shows that the hyperbolic maps are given by

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{O}}\colon\chow^{*}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\chern]/(n\chern)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\GW(k)[U,H,e]/(\ldots)\cong\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}h

\displaystyle\chern^{i}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\begin{cases}he^{i}=2e^{i}&$i$ even\\
He^{i}&$i$ odd\end{cases}

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{O}(1)}\colon\chow^{*}(B\mu_{n})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\chern]/(n\chern)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\GW(k)[U,H,e]/(\ldots)\cong\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mu_{n})

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}H

\displaystyle\chern^{i}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\begin{cases}He^{i}&$i$ even\\
he^{i}=2e^{i}&$i$ odd\end{cases}

This shows that the groups
\chowwitt^{i}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(i+1))
which are generated by
He^{i}
become trivial in
I^{j}
-cohomology.
For
i\geq 1
, this further implies

H^{i}(B\mu_{n},I^{i},\mathcal{O}(i))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2n\langle e^{i}\rangle/2e^{i}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\langle e^{i}\rangle\cong\witt(k)/I(k)\langle e^{i}\rangle\,.

The relation
nHe
is a multiple of
he=2e
and thus no longer appears.
In degree
0
and trivial twist compute

H^{0}(B\mu_{n},I^{0},\mathcal{O})\cong\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})/h\cong(\GW(k)\oplus\witt(k)\langle U\rangle)/h\cong\witt(k)\langle 1,U\rangle\,.

The statement follows by adding up these groups and inheriting the multiplication and relations from the Chow-Witt ring.
∎

Bemerkung 3.11
.

Let the base field
k
contain the real numbers
\mathbb{R}
.
Our scheme approximations of
B\mu_{n}
are not cellular, thus the condition under which
[
HWXZ21
, Theorem 5.7]
proves that the real cycle class map

H^{i}(B\mu_{n},I^{j},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H_{\mathrm{sing}}^{i}(B\mu_{n}(\mathbb{R}),\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{L}))

is an isomorphism for
j=i
is not met.

If
n
is even, this is in fact not an isomorphism:
The real realization, i.e. taking the set of real points of a scheme equipped with the analytic topology, commutes with products and quotients.
This means that the real realization of
E_{n}^{r}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)
which is a line bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{r}
, is again a real line bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{r}(\mathbb{R})=\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{r}
, and in case
n
is even this is orientable.
The zero section is also preserved under real realization, and removing the zero section of an oriented line bundle over
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{r}
divides the total space into two connected components which are both homotopy equivalent to
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{r}
.
Finally, taking the colimit over our approximations of increasing dimensions also commutes with real realization, thus
B_{\mathrm{gm}}\mu_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is homotopy equivalent to
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\amalg\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
.
Its (untwisted) singular cohomology ring is

H_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(B\mu_{n}(\mathbb{R});\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{\mathrm{sing}}^{*}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})\oplus H^{*}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}[x]/2x\oplus\mathbb{Z}[y]/2y

where
x
and
y
live in degree
2
and this is not isomorphic to the untwisted part of the
I^{j}
-cohomology we have computed above.
The real cycle class map is, however, an isomorphism for
j\geq i+3
:
Jacobson
[
Jac17
, Corollary 8.3]
proves this for
j\geq\dim X
.
By
Corollary

2.42
we can use
E_{n}^{r}
to compute
H^{i}(B\mu_{n},I^{j},\mathcal{L})
if
r\geq i+2
and this scheme has dimension
r+1\geq i+3
.
What happens in these bidegrees is roughly that the last term of the localization sequence
4
does not vanish, contributing an additional generator
Ue^{i}
to the Milnor-Witt and thus also the
I^{j}
-cohomology group.

If
n
is odd, the space of real points
B\mu_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is contractible with cohomology ring
\mathbb{Z}\cong\witt(\mathbb{R})
concentrated in degree
0
, thus coinciding with our results for
I^{j}
-cohomology.

Chapter 4
The
I^{j}
-Cohomology of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}

14
Fasel’s Projective Bundle Formula

Let
X
be a smooth scheme,
E
a vector bundle of rank
r
on
X
and
p\colon\mathbb{P}(E)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
the associated projective bundle.
Denoting by
\mathcal{E}
the locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module associated to
E
, there is a
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(E)}
-module
\mathcal{G}_{E}
defined by the short exact sequence

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{G}_{E}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}p^{*}\mathcal{E}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(E)}(1)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0\,.

We denote the total space of the vector bundle associated to
\mathcal{G}_{E}
by
G_{E}
.
Further let
\mathcal{L}
be a line bundle on
X
.
For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
denote by
\mathcal{L}(a)
the line bundle
p^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(E)}(a)
over
\mathbb{P}(E)
.
Recall that by quadratic periodicity (
Proposition

2.28
),
I^{j}
-cohomology twisted by
\mathcal{L}(a)
is isomorphic to that twisted in
\mathcal{L}
if
a
is even and
\mathcal{L}(-1)
if
a
is odd.

In
[
Fas13
]
, Fasel constructs maps

\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}\colon H^{i}(X,\overline{I}^{j})\xrightarrow{p^{*}}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),\overline{I}^{j})\xrightarrow{\bockstein_{\mathcal{L}(-1)}}H^{i+1}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j+1},\mathcal{L}(-1))\\
\xrightarrow{\cdot\euleri(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(E)}(1))^{a-1}}H^{i+a}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j+a},\mathcal{L}(-a))

for
a\geq 1
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\bockstein_{\mathcal{L}(-1)}
is the Bockstein introduced in
Eq.

2
, and

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{even}}^{\mathcal{L}}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq r-1\\
a\;\mathrm{even}\end{subarray}}\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\colon\bigoplus_{a}H^{i-a}(X,\overline{I}^{j-a})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\mathcal{L}}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq a\leq r-1\\
a\;\mathrm{odd}\end{subarray}}\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\colon\bigoplus_{a}H^{i-a}(X,\overline{I}^{j-a})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L}(-1))

for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
These two maps are split injective by
[
Fas13
, Cor. 5.8]
.

Definition 4.1

(Fasel)
.

For a vector bundle
E\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
and its associated projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(E)
, define the groups

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\coker\Theta_{\mathrm{even}}^{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L}(-1))

\displaystyle\coloneqq\coker\Theta_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\mathcal{L}}\,.

Fasel calls this group the reduced
I^{j}
-cohomology, but we will not adopt this because we are using the term to describe
\overline{I}^{j}
-cohomology.
Note also that the tilde in this definition has nothing to do with the notation for Chow-Witt groups.

Denote by
\xi_{E}\in H^{r-1}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{r-1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(E)}(-r))
the orientation class of the projective bundle
\mathbb{P}(E)
associated to a trivial vector bundle
E
of rank
r
over
X
, as defined in
[
Fas13
, Definition 6.1]
.
By
[
Fas13
, Prop. 6.4]
the triviality condition on the vector bundle can be omitted if
r
is odd, as in this case
\xi
agrees with the Euler class of
G_{E}^{\vee}
.
Fasel’s main theorems
[
Fas13
, Theorems 9.1, 9.2, 9.4]
summarize to the following.

Proposition 4.2

(Fasel)
.

Let
X
be a scheme,
\mathcal{L}
a line bundle over
X
,
E
a vector bundle of rank
r
over
X
and
p\colon\mathbb{P}(E)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}X
the associated projective bundle, and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then if
r
is odd, the compositions

1.

H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{p^{*}}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L})\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L})
and

2.

H^{i-r+1}(X,I^{j-r+1},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{\cdot\xi\circ p^{*}}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\omega_{\mathbb{P}(E)/X})\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\omega_{\mathbb{P}(E)/X})

are isomorphisms of
\witt(k)
-algebras.
If
r
is even,

3.

\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L}(-1))=0
,

4.

and if
\mathcal{E}
is free then

H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})\oplus H^{i-r+1}(X,I^{j-r+1},\mathcal{L})\xrightarrow{p^{*}\oplus(\cdot\xi\circ p^{*})}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L})\\
\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathbb{P}(E),I^{j},p^{*}\mathcal{L})

is an isomorphism.

Together with the fact that the maps
\Theta_{\mathrm{even}}^{\mathcal{L}}
and
\Theta_{\mathrm{odd}}^{\mathcal{L}}
are split injective, this theorem yields a direct formula to compute the
I^{j}
-cohomology groups of a projective bundle.

Caution:
\mathbb{P}^{r}=\mathbb{P}(k^{r+1})
is the projective bundle associated to the trivial rank
r+1
vector bundle over
k
and thus a rank
r+1
projective bundle.

Before computing the
I^{j}
-cohomology of the product
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
we need to know the
I^{j}
-cohomology ring of a single copy of projective space
\mathbb{P}^{r}
over a base field
k
.
The additive structure is an immediate consequence of the previous theorem and the multiplicative structure is due to
[
Wen24
, Prop. 4.1]
.
The reduced
I^{j}
-cohomology ring follows for example from
[
Fas13
, Theorem 4.1]
.

Korollar 4.3

(Fasel, Wendt)
.

The total
I^{j}
-cohomology ring of projective space
\mathbb{P}^{r}
over a base field
k
is

\displaystyle H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})

\displaystyle\cong\witt(k)[\euleri,R]/(I(k)\cdot\euleri,\euleri^{r+1},\euleri\cdot R,R^{2})

as
\witt(k)
-algebras,
where
\euleri
corresponds to the class
\euleri(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(-1))\in H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{1},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(1))
and
R
corresponds to
\xi\in H^{r}(\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{r},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(r-1))
.
The reduced cohomology ring is

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{i})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}[\sw]/\sw^{r+1}

where
\sw
corresponds to the class
\sw(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(-1))\in H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{1})
.
The reduction morphism
\widetilde{\rho}\colon H^{\ast}(\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{\ast},-)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{\ast}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{\ast})
maps
\euleri
to
\sw
and
R
to
\sw^{r}
.

Wendt characterizes the class
\euleri
as
\bockstein_{\mathcal{O}(1)}(1)
.
That this is in fact the Euler class of
\mathcal{O}(-1)
follows from
Lemma

4.7
.

15
The
I^{j}
-Cohomology Ring of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}

Consider the product space
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
for
q,\,r\geq 1
.
By
[
EH16
, Prop. 1.30]
, the Picard group of a smooth scheme is isomorphic to its first Chow group, which in the case of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
is shown in
[
Tot14
, Theorem 2.12]
to be isomorphic to
\Pic(\mathbb{P}^{q})\times\Pic(\mathbb{P}^{r})\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
.

Notation 4.4
.

We denote the two projections by

\mathrm{pr}_{1}\colon\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}\text{ and }\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}\,.

Line bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
are of the form
\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{q}}(s)\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(t)
and we will use the shorthand notation

\mathcal{O}(s,t)\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{q}}(s)\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(t)\,.

I^{j}
-cohomology considers twists in
\Pic(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})/2\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
,
whose elements can be represented e.g. by the line bundles
\mathcal{O}(0,0)
,
\mathcal{O}(0,1)
,
\mathcal{O}(1,0)
and
\mathcal{O}(1,1)
.

Satz 4.5
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic coprime to
2
.
The total
I^{j}
-cohomology ring of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
is isomorphic to the following
(\mathbb{Z},\Pic(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})/2)
-graded
\witt(k)
-algebra:

\witt(k)[e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},R_{1},R_{2}]/(I(k)\cdot e_{1},I(k)\cdot e_{2},I(k)\cdot e_{3},e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2},\\
e_{1}^{q+1},e_{2}^{r+1},e_{1}R_{1},e_{2}R_{2},R_{1}^{2},R_{2}^{2},\\
e_{2}R_{1}-e_{1}^{q}e_{3},e_{1}R_{2}-e_{2}^{r}e_{3},e_{3}R_{1}-e_{1}^{q}e_{2},e_{3}R_{2}-e_{1}e_{2}^{r})

where

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\euleri(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{1},\mathcal{O}(1,0))

\displaystyle e_{2}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\euleri(\mathcal{O}(0,-1))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{1},\mathcal{O}(0,1))

\displaystyle e_{3}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\euleri(\mathcal{O}(-1,-1))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{1},\mathcal{O}(1,1))

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\xi_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}^{q+1}}

\displaystyle\in H^{q}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{q},\mathcal{O}(q+1,0))

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\xi_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{q}}^{r+1}}

\displaystyle\in H^{r}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{r},\mathcal{O}(0,r+1))\,.

To prove this theorem we will apply Fasel’s formula
Proposition

4.2
to the projective bundle

p\coloneqq\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}\cong\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}^{\oplus q+1})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}\,.

The reduced cohomology of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
can be computed immediately using
[
Fas13
, Theorem 4.1]
:

Proposition 4.6
.

\bigoplus_{i}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{i})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}[\overline{c}_{1},\overline{c}_{2}]/(\overline{c}_{1}^{q+1},\overline{c}_{2}^{r+1})

where

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\sw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{1})

\displaystyle\overline{c}_{2}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\sw(\mathcal{O}(0,-1))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{1})

To shorten notation, in the following we will use the symbols
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
,
e_{3}
,
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
,
\overline{c}_{1}
,
\overline{c}_{2}
to also denote their corresponding classes in the (reduced)
I^{j}
-cohomology ring.
Observe that

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(e_{1})

\displaystyle=\overline{c}_{1}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(e_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{c}_{2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(e_{3})

\displaystyle=\sw(\mathcal{O}(-1,-1))=\sw(\mathcal{O}(-1,0)\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,-1))=\overline{c}_{1}+\overline{c}_{2}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(R_{1})

\displaystyle=\overline{c}_{1}^{q}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\rho}(R_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{c}_{2}^{r}\,.

where the third row uses the formula for Stiefel-Whitney classes of tensor products of line bundles, and the rest follows by pulling the respective classes back to one factor of the product where the reduction morphism is already known.

The classes
\sw^{i}
for
0\leq i\leq r
generate
H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})
as a bi-graded
\witt(k)
-module
and thus their images under the maps

p^{*},\,\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}\text{ and }(\cdot\xi_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}^{q+1}})\circ p^{*}=(\cdot R_{1})\circ p^{*}\colon H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{*})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{*})

appearing in Fasel’s formula produce a generating set of
H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})
.
Our strategy will be to express all of these in terms of the classes
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
,
e_{3}
,
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
to show that those generate
H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*},-)
as a
\witt(k)
-algebra.
For the pullback map
p^{*}
we simply have
p^{*}\sw^{i}=\overline{c}_{2}^{i}
, and likewise
R_{1}\cdot p^{*}(\sw^{i})=R_{1}\cdot\overline{c}_{2}^{i}
.
The main obstacle is to understand the Bockstein homomorphism
\bockstein_{\mathcal{O}(1,t)}
that occurs as a component of
\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}
.
We will use the following lemma of Fasel
[
Fas13
, Lemma 3.1]
.

Lemma 4.7

(Fasel)
.

Let
X
be a smooth scheme and
\mathcal{L}
,
\mathcal{M}
line bundles over
X
.
Let
\alpha\in H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})
.
Then the Bockstein homomorphism

\bockstein_{\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\vee}}\colon H^{i}(X,\overline{I}^{i})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i+1}(X,I^{i+1},\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{\vee})

maps
\widetilde{\rho}(\alpha)
to
\alpha\cdot e(\mathcal{M})
.

To compute
\bockstein_{\mathcal{O}(s,t)}(\overline{c}_{1}^{k}\overline{c}_{2}^{l})
, insert
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(k,l)
,
\alpha=e_{1}^{k}e_{2}^{l}\in H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{i},\mathcal{O}(k,l))
and
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{O}(k-s,l-t)
and obtain

\bockstein_{\mathcal{O}(s,t)}(\overline{c}_{1}^{k}\overline{c}_{2}^{l})=\bockstein_{\mathcal{O}(s,t)}(\rho_{\mathcal{O}(k,l)}(e_{1}^{k}e_{2}^{l}))=e_{1}^{k}e_{2}^{l}\cdot\euleri(\mathcal{O}(k-s,l-t))\,.

Note that Euler classes of squares vanish in
I^{j}
-cohomology, hence only the parity of
k-s
and
l-t
is relevant.

In the projective bundle formula there appear only Bocksteins whose twist is tautological on the first factor of the product, and they are composed with the map
p^{*}\colon H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{i})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{i})
whose image is generated by
p^{*}c^{i}=\overline{c}_{2}^{i}
.
So the relevant values are:

\displaystyle\bockstein_{\mathcal{O}(1,i)}(\overline{c}_{2}^{i})

\displaystyle=e_{1}e_{2}^{i}

\displaystyle\bockstein_{\mathcal{O}(1,i+1)}(\overline{c}_{2}^{i})

\displaystyle=e_{2}^{i}e_{3}

and thus

\displaystyle\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(i)}(\sw^{i})

\displaystyle=e_{1}^{a}e_{2}^{i}

\displaystyle\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(i+1)}(\sw^{i})

\displaystyle=e_{1}^{a-1}e_{2}^{i}e_{3}\,.

Having determined the images of the maps
p^{*}
,
\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}
and
\cdot R_{1}
appearing in Fasel’s formula yields a set of generators of all diagonal
I^{j}
-cohomology groups of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
as
\witt(k)
-modules.
By describing all of these as products of
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},R_{1},R_{2}
it follows that these five classes generate the total
I^{j}
-cohomology ring as a
\witt(k)
-algebra.

Concerning relations, there are
e_{1}^{q+1}
,
e_{1}R_{1}
,
R_{1}^{2}
as well as
e_{2}^{r+1}
,
e_{2}R_{2}
,
R_{2}^{2}
which are pulled back from the individual factors.
Since all other possible products of
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
,
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
generate their own summands in the
I^{j}
-cohomology groups of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
there are no new relations between these four generators.

So it remains to determine the relations involving
e_{3}
.
Since
e_{3}
lives in

\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{1},\mathcal{O}(1,1))

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(-1)}H^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{0})

\displaystyle=\mu_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(-1)}(1)\cdot\witt(k)/I(k)

\displaystyle=e_{3}\cdot\witt(k)/I(k)

one can deduce the relation
I(k)\cdot e_{3}
.
From our computations of the
\mu_{a}^{\mathcal{L}}
deduce further that the
e_{1}^{i}e_{2}^{j}e_{3}
for
0\leq i<q
and
0\leq j<r
each generate their own summand and thus are not subject to any relations.
To determine
e_{3}^{2}
consider its reduction:

\widetilde{\rho}(e_{3}^{2})=\widetilde{\rho}(e_{3})^{2}=(\overline{c}_{1}+\overline{c}_{2})^{2}=\overline{c}_{1}^{2}+2\overline{c}_{1}\overline{c}_{2}+\overline{c}_{2}^{2}=\overline{c}_{1}^{2}+\overline{c}_{2}^{2}

The last equality holds because
2\in I(k)
vanishes in
I(k)
-torsion.
Since
e_{3}^{2}
lives in
H^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{2},\mathcal{O})
which by the projective bundle formula equals
\witt(k)/I(k)\cdot e_{1}^{2}\oplus\witt(k)/I(k)\cdot e_{2}^{2}
,
the only possible preimage of
\overline{c}_{1}^{2}+\overline{c}_{2}^{2}
is
e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}
.
Therefore
e_{3}^{2}=e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}
.
Similar arguments yield
e_{1}^{q}e_{3}=e_{2}R_{1}
,
e_{2}^{r}e_{3}=e_{1}R_{2}
,
e_{3}R_{1}=e_{1}^{q}e_{2}
and
e_{3}R_{2}=e_{1}e_{2}^{r}
.
Now we have covered all possible monomials involving
e_{3}
and thus determined all relations, finishing the computation.

Korollar 4.8
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic coprime to
2
.
The total
I^{j}
-cohomology ring of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
is isomorphic to the following
(\mathbb{Z},\Pic(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})/2)
-graded
\witt(k)
-algebra:

\witt(k)[e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}]/(I(k)\cdot e_{1},I(k)\cdot e_{2},I(k)\cdot e_{3},e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2})

where

\displaystyle e_{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\euleri(\mathcal{O}(-1,0))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},I^{1},\mathcal{O}(1,0))

\displaystyle e_{2}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\euleri(\mathcal{O}(0,-1))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}I^{1},\mathcal{O}(0,1))

\displaystyle e_{3}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}\euleri(\mathcal{O}(-1,-1))

\displaystyle\in H^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},I^{1},\mathcal{O}(1,1))\,.

Proof.

This follows from the computation of
H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})
in
Theorem

4.5
and the approximation of classifying spaces in
Corollaries

2.42
and
2.43
.

\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
is an approximation for
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
in degrees
<\min(q,r)-1
.
The classes
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
as well as all relations involving
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
,
e_{1}^{q}
or
e_{2}^{r}
live in degrees above this boundary and thus don’t appear here.
∎

Bemerkung 4.9
.

The considered schemes
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
are all cellular and thus the real cycle class map

H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(X(\mathbb{R}),\mathbb{Z}(\mathcal{L}))

is an isomorphism for all
i
by
[
HWXZ21
, Theorem 5.7]
.
Compare this for example with the computation of
[
Hat01
, Example 3.E.5]
of the singular cohomology of
\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}
in (non-twisted)
\mathbb{Z}
-coefficients:

H^{*}(\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty}\times\mathbb{R}\mathbb{P}^{\infty};\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}[\lambda,\mu,\nu]/(2\lambda,2\mu,2\nu,\nu^{2}+\lambda^{2}\mu+\lambda\mu^{2})

with
\lambda
and
\mu
living in degree
2
and
\nu
in degree
3
.
This embeds as the untwisted subring of the total
I^{j}
-cohomology ring of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
via

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}e_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}e_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\nu

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}e_{1}e_{2}e_{3}

and the relation

\nu^{2}+\lambda^{2}\mu+\lambda\mu^{2}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}e_{1}^{2}e_{2}^{2}e_{3}^{2}+e_{1}^{4}e_{2}^{2}+e_{1}^{2}e_{2}^{4}=e_{1}^{2}e_{2}^{2}(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}+e_{3}^{2})

is respected since
e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}+e_{3}^{2}=0
.

16
I^{j}
-Cohomology in Non-Diagonal Bidegrees

The computations in the following chapters will involve
H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{j},\mathcal{L})
for
j=i-1
.
It turns out that this group is straightforward to compute for
j<i
which we will do in this section.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
j<i
.

H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{j},\mathcal{L})\cong\begin{cases}\witt(k)&$i=0$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(0,0)$\\
\witt(k)\oplus\witt(k)&$i=q=r$ odd, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(0,0)$\\
\witt(k)&$i=r$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(0,r-1)$ and [$r\neq q$ or $r$ even]\\
\witt(k)&$i=q$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(q-1,0)$ and [$q\neq r$ or $q$ even]\\
\witt(k)&$i=q+r$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(q-1,r-1)$\\
0&else\\
\end{cases}

Proof.

Observe that for
j<i
,
I^{j-i}=\witt(k)
by definition and
\overline{I}^{j-i}=0
.
This implies that in these cases the
I^{j}
-cohomology groups are isomorphic to the groups
\widetilde{H}^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{j},\mathcal{L})
as defined in
Definition

4.1
.

Recall:

H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{j},\mathcal{L})\cong\begin{cases}\witt(k)&$i=0$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}$\\
\witt(k)&$i=r$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(r-1)$\\
0&else\end{cases}

The statement then follows immediately from the projective bundle theorem (
Proposition

4.2
).
∎

Chapter 5
The Chow-Witt Ring of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}

17
The Chow-Witt Ring of
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
and
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}

According to
Proposition

2.22
there is an isomorphism

c\colon\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}

where

\partial_{\mathcal{L}}^{i}\colon\chow^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i+1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{i+1},\mathcal{L})

is the boundary map of the long exact cohomology sequence associated to the sequence

of chain complexes.
In order to compute this kernel, first compute the Chow ring in which it lives using a result of Totaro about Chow rings of products:

Proposition 5.1

(
[
Tot14
, Lemma 2.3, Theorem 2.12]
)
.

\displaystyle\chow^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{r})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}[\chern]/(\chern^{r+1})

\displaystyle\chow^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{Z}[c_{1},c_{2}]/(c_{1}^{q+1},c_{2}^{r+1})

\displaystyle\chow^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})/2

\displaystyle\cong H^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},\overline{I}^{*})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}[\sw_{1},\sw_{2}]/(\sw_{1}^{q+1},\sw 2^{r+1})

According to the
“key diagram”
explained in
Section

3
,
\partial_{\mathcal{L}}
equals
\bockstein_{\mathcal{L}}\circ\modulo 2
.
We have already computed this Bockstein
\bockstein_{\mathcal{L}}
in the previous section after
Lemma

4.7
, so now it is straightforward to describe its kernel.
From this explicit description one checks that for
\alpha\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\beta\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{M}}
, the product
\alpha\beta
lies in
\ker\partial_{\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{M}}
and thus the kernels assemble into the
\mathbb{Z}\times(\Pic/2)
-graded ring

\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}[C_{1},C_{2},C_{3},r_{1},r_{2},h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}]/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{K})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})/2}\ker\partial_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}c_{1}\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(1,0)}^{1}

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}c_{2}\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(0,1)}^{1}

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}c_{1}+c_{2}\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(1,1)}^{1}

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}c_{1}^{q}\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(q+1,0)}^{q}

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}c_{2}^{r}\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(0,r+1)}^{r}

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}2\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(1,0)}^{0}

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}2\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(0,1)}^{0}

\displaystyle h_{3}

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}2\in\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(1,1)}^{0}

where

is the ideal generated by

\displaystyle h_{1}^{2}-4,h_{2}^{2}-4,h_{3}^{2}-4,

\displaystyle h_{1}h_{2}-2h_{3},h_{2}h_{3}-2h_{1},h_{1}h_{3}-2h_{2},

\displaystyle 2C_{1}+h_{3}C_{2}-h_{2}C_{3},h_{1}C_{1}+h_{2}C_{2}-h_{3}C_{3},h_{2}C_{1}+h_{1}C_{2}-2C_{3},h_{3}C_{1}+2C_{2}-h_{1}C_{3}

and

is the ideal generated by

\displaystyle C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2}+h_{3}C_{1}C_{2}-C_{3}^{2},

\displaystyle C_{1}^{q}C_{3}-C_{2}r_{1},C_{2}^{r}C_{3}-C_{1}r_{2},C_{3}r_{1}-C_{1}^{q}C_{2},C_{3}r_{2}-C_{1}C_{2}^{r}\,.

Note that
\ker\partial_{\mathcal{O}(0,0)}
also contains

and

So now we can apply

is generated by the relations

\displaystyle H_{1}^{2}-2h,H_{2}^{2}-2h,H_{3}^{2}-2h,

(7)

\displaystyle H_{1}H_{2}-2H_{3},H_{2}H_{3}-2H_{1},H_{1}H_{3}-2H_{2},

(8)

(9)

Note that

,
and the classes

,

are the images of

under the hyperbolic maps

for

They can be thought of as twisted versions of the hyperbolic form

.

Proof.

We will use the fiber product formula from
Proposition

2.22
.
The fiber product of two
\GW(k)
-algebras contains precisely those pairs in the set-wise product where both entries have the same image in
\chowmodtwo^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})
.
This entire proof consists of spelling out that set-wise characterization.

Recall that we have a set of generators
C_{1},\,C_{2},\,C_{3},\,h_{1},\,h_{2},\,h_{3},\,r_{1},\,r_{2}
of
\oplus_{\mathcal{L}}\ker\partial_{\mathcal{L}}
and
e_{1},\,e_{2},\,e_{3},\,R_{1},\,R_{2}
of
H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})
as
\GW(k)
-algebras.
Thus as
\GW(k)
-modules, these two are generated by all monomials of the form
h_{1}^{i_{1}}h_{2}^{i_{2}}h_{3}^{i_{3}}C_{1}^{i_{4}}C_{2}^{i_{5}}C_{3}^{i_{6}}r_{1}^{i_{7}}r_{2}^{i_{8}}
respectively
e_{1}^{j_{4}}e_{2}^{j_{5}}e_{3}^{j_{6}}R_{1}^{j_{7}}R_{2}^{j_{8}}
where the exponents are non-negative integers.
Observe that monomials of the first form are mapped to zero in
\chowmodtwo^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})
if and only if the monomial vanishes in
\oplus_{\mathcal{L}}\ker\partial_{\mathcal{L}}
already or if at least one of
i_{1},\,i_{2},\,i_{3}
is not zero.
A monomial of the second form is in the kernel of
\widetilde{\rho}
if and only if it already vanishes in
H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})
.

To determine for a given monomial
\alpha
of the first form all pairs in the fiber product that have
\alpha
as second coordinate, compute the
\modulo 2
-reduction
\overline{\alpha}
and its preimage
\widetilde{\rho}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})
in the
I^{j}
-cohomology ring.
The latter can be read off from our description of
H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})
in
Theorem

4.5
.

If
\alpha=0
or one of the exponents
i_{1},\,i_{2},\,i_{3}
is non-zero, then
\widetilde{\rho}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})=\ker(\widetilde{\rho})
.
Otherwise if
\alpha=C_{1}^{i_{4}}C_{2}^{i_{5}}C_{3}^{i_{6}}r_{1}^{i_{7}}r_{2}^{i_{8}}
we find that
\widetilde{\rho}^{-1}(\overline{\alpha})=e_{1}^{i_{4}}e_{2}^{i_{5}}e_{3}^{i_{6}}R_{1}^{i_{7}}R_{2}^{i_{8}}+\ker(\widetilde{\rho})
.
Hence the following form a set of generators of the fiber product as a
\GW(k)
-module.

•

(0,h_{1}^{i_{1}}h_{2}^{i_{2}}h_{3}^{i_{3}}C_{1}^{i_{4}}C_{2}^{i_{5}}C_{3}^{i_{6}}r_{1}^{i_{7}}r_{2}^{i_{8}})
if one out of
i_{1},\,i_{2},\,i_{3}
non-zero

•

(e_{1}^{i_{4}}e_{2}^{i_{5}}e_{3}^{i_{6}}R_{1}^{i_{7}}R_{2}^{i_{8}},C_{1}^{i_{4}}C_{2}^{i_{5}}C_{3}^{i_{6}}r_{1}^{i_{7}}r_{2}^{i_{8}})
for
i_{4},\,i_{5},\,i_{6},\,i_{7},\,i_{8}
any non-negative integers such that the second entry of the pair is not zero

•

(\beta,0)
with
\beta\in\ker(\widetilde{\rho})

The third case, however, is redundant:
Any
\beta\in\ker(\widetilde{\rho})
can be expressed as a
\witt(k)
-linear combination of monomials
e_{1}^{j_{4}}e_{2}^{j_{5}}e_{3}^{j_{6}}R_{1}^{j_{7}}R_{2}^{j_{8}}
with coefficients
\beta_{j_{4},\ldots,j_{8}}
in
I(k)\subseteq\witt(k)
.
Now if
\beta_{j_{4},\ldots,j_{8}}
is in
I(k)
, then the pair
(\beta_{j_{4},\ldots,j_{8}},0)
is in the fiber product
\witt(k)\times_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathbb{Z}\cong\GW(k)
and therefore one can write

(\beta,0)=\sum_{j_{4},\ldots,j_{8}}(\beta_{j_{4},\ldots,j_{8}},0)\cdot(e_{1}^{j_{4}}e_{2}^{j_{5}}e_{3}^{j_{6}}R_{1}^{j_{7}}R_{2}^{j_{8}},C_{1}^{j_{4}}C_{2}^{j_{5}}C_{3}^{j_{6}}r_{1}^{j_{7}}r_{2}^{j_{8}})

thus reducing to the second case.

All pairs in the first two cases can be expressed as monomials in the following eight pairs, which therefore form a set of generators of the fiber product as a
\GW(k)
-
algebra
.
We assign the symbols on the left-hand side to the corresponding elements in the Chow-Witt ring.

\displaystyle c\colon\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\displaystyle H^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{*})\times_{\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}}\chowmodtwo^{*}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})}\bigoplus_{\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r})/2}\ker\partial_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(0,h_{1}),(0,h_{2}),(0,h_{3})

\displaystyle e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(e_{1},C_{1}),(e_{2},C_{2}),(e_{3},C_{3})

\displaystyle R_{1},R_{2}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(R_{1},r_{1}),(R_{2},r_{2})

Relations in the fiber product on the right hand side consist of pairs such that each coordinate is a relation in the respective factor. The fundamental ideal becomes

\displaystyle I(k)\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(I(k),0)\,

thus all pairs which contain
I(k)
in the first coordinate become relations in the fiber product, that is,

\displaystyle I(k)e_{1},I(k)e_{2},I(k)e_{3}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(I(k),0)\cdot(e_{1},C_{1})=(I(k)e_{1},0)=(0,0),\,\ldots

Further one finds relations

\displaystyle I(k)H_{1},I(k)H_{2},I(k)H_{3}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(I(k),0)\cdot(0,h_{1}),\,\ldots

The relations in
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
live in degree
0
and
1
where the only non-trivial relations in the
I^{*}
-cohomology ring are
I(k)e_{i}
which are already covered above. Thus each of these has a straightforward analogue in the Chow-Witt ring

\displaystyle H_{1}^{2}-2h=H_{1}^{2}-h^{2},\,\ldots\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(0,h_{1}^{2})-(0,4),\,\ldots

\displaystyle he_{1}+H_{3}e_{2}-H_{2}e_{3}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(0,2)(e_{1},C_{1})+(0,h_{3})(e_{2},C_{2})-(0,h_{2})(e_{3},C_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(0,2C_{1}+h_{3}C_{2}-h_{2}C_{3})

and we will denote the ideal generated by these by
\mathcal{J}
. Something interesting happens with the relation
e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2}
. There are several pairs in the fiber product containing this in the first coordinate, the most obvious one being

\displaystyle e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2},C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2}-C_{3}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2},C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2}-(C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2}+h_{3}C_{1}C_{2}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2},-h_{3}C_{1}C_{2})

whose second coordinate is not a relation in the Chow ring. Only subtracting
(0,-h_{3}C_{1}C_{2})
yields the following relation.

\displaystyle e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}+H_{3}e_{1}e_{2}-e_{3}^{2}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2},C_{1}^{2}+C_{2}^{2}+h_{3}C_{1}C_{2}-C_{3}^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}-e_{3}^{2},0)

All other relations from
Theorem

4.5
appear in a unique pair in the fiber product whose second coordinate is in
\mathcal{K}
, thus each yields one relation:

\displaystyle e_{1}^{q+1},\,\ldots\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{q+1},C_{1}^{q+1}),\,\ldots

\displaystyle e_{1}^{q}e_{3}-e_{2}R_{1},\,\ldots\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle(e_{1}^{q}e_{3}-e_{2}R_{1},C_{1}^{q}C_{3}-C_{2}r_{1}),\,\ldots\qed

Korollar 5.3
.

For
k
a perfect field of characteristic coprime to
2
, there is an isomorphism of graded
\GW(k)
-algebras

\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}
}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 1.25 0 C 1.25 0.69 0.69 1.25 0 1.25 C -0.69 1.25 -1.25 0.69 -1.25 0 C -1.25 -0.69 -0.69 -1.25 0 -1.25 C 0.69 -1.25 1.25 -0.69 1.25 0 Z M 0 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})\cong\GW(k)[e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},H_{1},H_{2},H_{3}]/(I(k)\cdot(H_{1},H_{2},H_{3},e_{1},e_{2},e_{3})\\
e_{1}^{2}+e_{2}^{2}+H_{3}e_{1}e_{2}-e_{3}^{2},\mathcal{J})

with the symbols on the right hand side corresponding to the same classes as in
Corollary

5.2
.

Proof.

We have already observed that
(V_{1}^{r+1}\smallsetminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}\cong\mathbb{P}^{r}
is an approximation of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
in codimension
<r
in the sense of
Proposition

2.37
.
By
Corollary

2.43
the product space
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
is an approximation of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
in codimension
<\min{q,r}-1
.

In the description of
Corollary

5.2
the classes
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
,
e_{3}
,
H_{1}
,
H_{2}
,
H_{3}
remain stable for all
q,r\geq 2
and thus survive in the Chow-Witt ring of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
The classes
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
on the other hand live in codimension
q
respectively
r
which is not in the range where
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
is an approximation for
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
The same holds for all relations involving
e_{1}^{q}
or
e_{2}^{r}
.
∎

18
Milnor-Witt
K
-Theory Groups in Non-Diagonal Bidegrees

For the computations in
Chapter

6
we will also need to know the group
H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})
for
j\in\{i-1,i-2\}
.
To this end observe that the argument of
[
HW19
, Prop. 2.11]
extends to a statement about Milnor-Witt cohomology in non-diagonal bidegrees.
In this case the key diagram of
[
HW19
]
reads as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,I^{j+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(X,I^{j+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,I^{j+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j}/2)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(X,I^{j+1},\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{i+1}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\modulo h_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\modulo 2}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bockstein}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

Proposition 5.4
.

The canonical group homomorphism

\phi\coloneqq(\modulo h_{\mathcal{L}})\times\rho\colon H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ker\delta\times_{H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j}/2)}\ker\partial

is always surjective.
It is injective if one of the following conditions hold:

1.

H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})
has no non-trivial
2
-torsion.

2.

The map
\eta\colon H^{i}(X,I^{j+1},\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})
is injective.

If further
H^{i+1}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})
has no non-trivial
2
-torsion, then
\ker\delta
equals
H^{i}(X,I^{j},\mathcal{L})
.

Proof.

The existence of the group homomorphism
\alpha
is implied by the universal property of the fiber product.

For surjectivity of
\phi
consider an element
(\alpha,\beta)\in\ker\delta\times_{H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j}/2)}\ker\partial
.
Choose an arbitrary lift
\widetilde{\alpha}\in H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})
of
\alpha
and denote the image of this lift in
\ker\partial
by
\gamma
.
The reduction of
\gamma
agrees with the reduction of
\beta
,
thus
\beta-\gamma
has a lift
\widetilde{\beta}\in H^{i}(X,K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})
.
Then
\widetilde{\alpha}+h_{\mathcal{L}}(\widetilde{\beta})
is in the preimage of
(\alpha,\beta)
under
\phi
.

Assume condition (1) holds and consider an element
\varepsilon\in\ker\phi=\ker\rho\,\cap\,\ker(\modulo h_{\mathcal{L}})
.
By exactness this must have a lift
\widetilde{\varepsilon}
along
h_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Due to commutativity of the upper square in the key diagram
\widetilde{\varepsilon}
must be
2
-torsion and thus trivial by assumption,
hence
\varepsilon=0
.
This proves injectivity.
Under condition (2) choose a lift
\widetilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
in
H^{i}(X,I^{j+1},\mathcal{L})
.
Then
\widetilde{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
must be zero due to injectivity of
\eta
and commutativity of the left-most square, and thus
\varepsilon=0
.

The last statement about
\ker\delta
follows from the commutativity of the top square in the diagram:
If multiplication with
2
is injective, then so is
\rho\circ h_{\mathcal{L}}
and thus
h_{\mathcal{L}}
.
Since
\delta
sits in the left vertical exact sequence before
h_{\mathcal{L}}
, it follows that
\delta=0
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

For
j<i
:

\displaystyle H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\witt(k)&$i=0$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}$\\
\witt(k)\langle R_{1},R_{2}\rangle&$i=q=r$ odd, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(0,0)$\\
\witt(k)\langle R_{1}\rangle&$i=q$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(q-1,0)$ and [$q\neq r$ or $q$ even]\\
\witt(k)\langle R_{2}\rangle&$i=r$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(0,r-1)$ and [$q\neq r$ or $r$ even]\\
\witt(k)\langle R_{1}\cdot R_{2}\rangle&$i=q+r$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}(q-1,r-1)$\\
0&else\end{cases}

\displaystyle H^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\witt(k)&$i=0$, $\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}$\\
0&else\end{cases}

Proof.

Note that
H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})
vanishes for
j<i
(as follows e.g. from
[
Tot14
, Theorem 2.12]
) thus condition (1) of the fiber product formula
Proposition

5.4
is satisfied, and its subgroup
\ker\partial
vanishes as well.
Hence
H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{j},\mathcal{L})\cong\ker\delta\subseteq H^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},I^{j},\mathcal{L})
where the latter has been computed in
Proposition

4.10
.
Since
H^{i+1}(\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r},K^{\mathrm{M}}_{j})
also vanishes, the last inclusion is in fact an equality.

For the statement on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, use the approximation of equivariant Chow-Witt groups from
Corollary

2.43
.
∎

Chapter 6
Chow-Witt Ring of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}

Throughout this chapter, let
n
be a positive integer, and
k
a perfect field with characteristic coprime to
2
and
n
.

The Picard group of
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
is isomorphic to its first Chow group by
[
EH16
, Prop. 1.30]
which is computed in
[
Tot14
, Theorem 2.10, Lemma 2.12]
to be isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/n
.
For line bundles on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
(or more precisely, its scheme approximations) we adopt the same notation

\mathcal{O}(s,t)=\mathrm{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{B\mathbb{G}_{m}}(s)\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{B\mu_{n}}(t)

as for bundles on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
Let
E_{n}^{r}
be the approximation of
B\mu_{n}
constructed in
Chapter

3
and consider the vector bundle
\pi\colon\mathbb{P}^{q}\times E_{n}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
.
Let
\mathcal{L}
be a line bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times E_{n}^{r}
.
Consider the localization sequence associated to the closed subscheme

\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}\xhookrightarrow{(\mathrm{id},s_{0})}\mathbb{P}^{q}\times E_{n}^{r}\xleftarrow{(\mathrm{id},\iota)}\mathbb{P}^{q}\times(E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{r}))\,.

Using the same arguments as in
Theorem

3.7
— scheme approximations of classifying spaces, the isomorphism
\det\Omega_{\mathbb{P}^{q}\times E_{n}^{r}/\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}}^{\vee}\cong\det\Omega_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)/\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}}^{\vee}\cong\mathcal{O}(0,n)
of line bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}
, and homotopy invariance — for
q
and
r
sufficiently large this sequence is isomorphic to

\ldots\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{i-1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,-n))\xrightarrow{\cdot\eulercw(\pi)}\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L})\\
\xrightarrow{\iota^{*}}\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\iota^{*}\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{i-1},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,n))\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\ldots

(10)

As already shown in
Chapter

3
the line bundle
E_{n}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{r}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)
.
Thus
\mathbb{P}^{q}\times E_{n}^{r}
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}}(n)=\mathcal{O}(0,n)
and therefore the map
\cdot\eulercw(\pi)
can also be expressed as
\cdot\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(0,n))
.
This Euler class can be computed using
Proposition

2.45
:

\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(0,n)))=\begin{cases}-\frac{n}{2}\cdot h_{\mathcal{O}}(c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(0,1)))=-H_{2}e_{2}&$n$ even\\
-\frac{n}{2}he_{2}=-ne_{2}&$n$ odd\end{cases}

19
Group Structure

Satz 6.1
.

For
\mathcal{L}
any line bundle on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
the following are isomorphisms of
\GW(k)
-modules.

If
n
is odd:

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\cong\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})/ne_{2}

If
n
is even:

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\cong\begin{cases}\GW(k)\oplus\witt(k)\langle U_{2}\rangle&$i=0$, $\mathcal{L}$ trivial\\
\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L})/\frac{n}{2}H_{2}e_{2}&else\end{cases}

Here
U_{2}
is the pullback of the class
U
from
Theorem

3.6
along the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}B\mu_{n}
onto the second factor, and

e_{2}
is the pullback of
e_{2}
defined in
Corollary

5.3
along
\pi
.

Since the bundle
\mathcal{O}(0,1)
on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
is defined as the pullback along
\pi
of the same bundle on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, and Euler classes commute with pullbacks,
e_{2}
is actually the Euler class of
\mathcal{O}(0,-1)
on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
.

Proof.

Except for
i=0
and trivial twist, the rightmost term in the localization sequence
Eq.

10
is shown to vanish in
Lemma

5.5
, proving the statement in these cases.

Assume that
n
is odd.
Then the localization sequence implies

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{L})

\displaystyle\cong\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L})/\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(0,n))\cdot\chowwitt^{i-1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,n))

\displaystyle\cong\chowwitt^{i}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L})/ne_{2}\cdot\chowwitt^{i-1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,n))\,.\Theta_{\mathrm{even}}^{\mathcal{L}}

In fact this argument also covers the case
i=0
with trivial twist, since over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
the line bundle
\mathcal{O}(1,0)
is trivial and thus

\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\cong\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(0,1))\,.

This works because
\mathcal{O}(1,0)
is trivial over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}
but not over
B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Now assume that
n
is even.
For
i=0
and trivial twist,
Lemma

5.5
shows that
H^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{O}\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,n))\cong\witt(k)
and
H^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{0},\mathcal{O})\cong 0
.
In the previous section we have computed
\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{O})\cong\GW(k)
.
Furthermore
\chowwitt^{-1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{O}\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,-n))
vanishes for degree reasons.
Thus the localization sequence
10
reads:

0\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\GW(k)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\witt(k)\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}0

Now consider the class
U\in\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})
constructed in
[
LM23
, Prop. 5.2.1]
and its pullback along the projection
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}B\mu_{n}
(or
\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon\mathbb{P}^{q}\times E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}E_{n}^{r}\smallsetminus s_{0}
to be precise).
This class is shown in
[
LM23
]
to define a split of the boundary morphism

\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n},\iota^{*}\mathcal{L})\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}H^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1},s_{0}^{*}\mathcal{L}\otimes\mathcal{O}(0,n))

of the localization sequence
Eq.

4
.
The map
\mathrm{pr}_{2}
together with its respective restrictions induces the following maps between the sequences
Eq.

4
and
Eq.

10
(all groups have trivial twist):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{0}(B\mu_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},K^{\mathrm{MW}}_{-1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot ne}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot ne_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{id}\times\iota)^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

This diagram commutes according to
Lemma

2.27
since we have

\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}N(s_{0}\colon\mathbb{P}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}E_{n}^{r})\cong\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r}(n)}\cong\mathcal{O}(0,n)\cong N(\mathrm{id}\times s_{0}\colon\mathbb{P}^{q}\times\mathbb{P}^{r}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathbb{P}^{q}\mathchoice{\longrightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}{\rightarrow}E_{n}^{r})\,.

The right-most vertical arrow is an isomorphism which follows from the projective bundle theorem (
Proposition

4.2
) together with the fact that these two Milnor-Witt cohomology groups are naturally isomorphic to the corresponding
I^{j}
-cohomology groups as explained in the proof of
Lemma

5.5
.
Together with commutativity of the right-hand square this shows that
\mathrm{pr}_{2}^{*}(U)\eqqcolon U_{2}
defines a split of the boundary map in the localization sequence
19
which proves the statement for
i=0
and
\mathcal{L}
trivial.
∎

Ring Structure

.

We have the following isomorphisms of graded
\GW(k)
-algebras.

If

is odd:

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}

\displaystyle H_{1}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle h_{\mathcal{O}(1,0)}(1)\in\chowwitt^{0}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O}(1,0))

\displaystyle e_{1}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle e_{2}\mathchoice{\longmapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}{\mapsto}

\displaystyle\eulercw(\mathcal{O}(0,1))\in\chowwitt^{1}(B\mathbb{G}_{m}\times B\mu_{n},\mathcal{O})

If

is even:

\displaystyle\chowwitt^{\hbox to2.21pt{\vbox to2.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.10417pt\lower-1.10417pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}{{{}}
